<meta>This page contains an introductory poem explaining the allegorical frontispiece (title plate) of the volume. It introduces Abraham as a central figure of virtue and faith, surrounded by personifications of vices he has overcome.</meta>

## ON THE FRONTISPIECE.

The Patriarch ABRAHAM, who, grave of countenance,
Stands upon the *Head* of the *Cornerstone* <note>A common Christian metaphor referring to Christ as the foundation of faith</note>, like an *Oak* of Mamre <note>The Oak of Mamre was the site where Abraham built an altar and received divine visitors</note>,
Whose *Weapon* served to loose Lot from Elam’s bonds <note>A reference to Genesis 14, where Abraham rescued his nephew Lot from the King of Elam</note>,
Just as his *Shepherd's Staff* watched over the cattle,
Faithfully embraces the true SAVING FAITH <note>original: "Heilgeloof"</note>:
Her *Sacrifice* makes him deaf to the flattery of passions;
While INCONSTANCY, whose unbound *Clothing*
Signifies fickle senses, like the *Globe* and *Feathers* <note>In 17th-century art, feathers and globes often symbolized instability and the fleeting nature of worldly things</note>;
IDOLATRY, who keeps her eye upon her *Household God*,
And SELF-LOVE, who gazes at her *False Jewelry*:
All these see themselves bridled by their Victor,
While OBEDIENCE, through her wings,
Makes his feet willing to undergo the *Yoke* of morals,
And swiftly strike the path of that *Law*, *Bridled*.
Thus he sees himself met by a Ray from Heaven,
And his *Possessions* and *Cattle* blessed upon Hebron’s mountains.

J. W.

<summary>This poem provides an allegorical interpretation of the book's frontispiece, depicting Abraham as a model of faith and obedience who triumphs over vices like Idolatry and Self-Love.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham, Allegory, Faith, Virtue vs Vice, Mamre, Frontispiece, Jan Wandelaar, Religious Emblems</keywords>

To bear your name on its fair forehead; <note>The "forehead" refers to the title page or frontispiece of the book, which proudly displays the patron's name.</note>

No mythical poem of foreign gods

The ancient Glory of the Hebrews,
The fame of more than thirty centuries,
God’s bosom friend, enlightened by God’s Spirit,
Is the subject of my Biblical poem.

# D E D I C A T I O N.

It is Abraham who, upon my pages,
With his Faith and Heroic Deeds,
As far as my poetic skill allows,
Is brought into the clear light of day.
Grant now to my poetry,
That she may dedicate this labor to you;
Let this word-painting <note>original: "Letterſchilderyë," a metaphor suggesting that the written word can "paint" a scene as vividly as a brush.</note> hang
In your private study; where neither Affairs of State
Nor fleeting worldly cares may intrude
Into your spiritual contemplations.
Thus may there rest upon you, and your lineage,
The blessing of my Abraham!
So shall your name endure through time,
Honored by the pilots of the Netherlands <note>original: "Palinuuren," a reference to Palinurus, the famous navigator from Virgil’s Aeneid, used here as a metaphor for the skilled statesmen who steer the Dutch "ship of state."</note>,
As long as the river Schie draws her breath,
And pays her tribute to the Maas! <note>The Schie is a river that flows through South Holland, eventually emptying into the river Maas; Hoogvliet uses this local geography to signify "as long as the rivers flow," or forever.</note>

*At Vlaardingen, the 15th*
*of the Harvest Month 1727.* <note>The "Harvest Month" (Herfstmaant) is the traditional Dutch name for September.</note>

->**Arnold Hoogvliet.**<-

# PREFACE.

I undertake to bring before the eyes of the modest Reader the most glorious example of Virtue and Piety found in either divine or human writings: **ABRAHAM THE PATRIARCH**; surely the most blessed and illustrious Personage (excepting, with all reverence, the Savior of the world, the Lord Jesus Christ) that the ages have ever seen. For who—to recall only sacred examples, for neither the Alexanders nor the Caesars <note>The author refers to Alexander the Great and Julius Caesar, the typical subjects of classical Greek and Roman epics, to argue that his biblical subject is more worthy of study than these secular "conquerors."</note> may be weighed here—who then, I say, of the divine Heroes of Faith obtained from the holy Writers so much renown for his untainted virtue or his pious and righteous conduct? Neither the divine Moses, the patient Job <note>original: "Hiob"</note>, the pious David, nor the wise Solomon obtained so many glorious names of praise and titles of honor. Upon whom do so many salutary promises rest? Whose name is, according to one of those very promises, so greatly glorified across the whole earth? A Name, which during his life [was] not

<meta>catchword: al-</meta>

<center>**</center>

<summary>In this preface, Hoogvliet introduces the patriarch Abraham as the ultimate human example of virtue, arguing that Abraham surpasses even other major biblical figures and secular historical heroes in his renown and divine favor.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham, Preface, virtue, piety, Jesus Christ, Moses, Job, David, Solomon, Alexander the Great, Julius Caesar, heroes of faith</keywords>

only spread so far among the surrounding pagan peoples that the Kings and Princes of Palestine recognized him as a divine Prince and sought his alliance from all sides (after he, through that famous march and glorious victory over the King of Elam <note>The author refers to the biblical account in Genesis 14, where Abraham defeated Chedorlaomer, King of Elam, to rescue his nephew Lot.</note>, had wiped the envy from their hearts, as if with the blood of those tyrants); but after his death, his name grew steadily greater, providing work for the pens of both sacred and secular historians. Through so many centuries until this very time, his name is preserved in blessed and reverent memory, not only among the Jews, who recognize Abraham as their physical forefather; and among all Christian nations, who recognize him as their spiritual Father of Faith; but even among the Muslims <note>original: "Mahomethanen"; a historical term for followers of Islam.</note>.

Of my great Hero of Faith, Abraham, mention is made not only by the oldest and most faithful historian, the divine Moses, and all the holy Prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles; but also by Jesus son of Sirach <note>Referring to the Book of Sirach or Ecclesiasticus in the Apocrypha.</note>; and the Jewish historian Josephus, who, at the beginning of his detailed account, brings forward the praiseworthy testimonies regarding Abraham from the Chaldean historian Berosus <note>Berosus was a 3rd-century BCE Babylonian writer; Josephus cites him to provide external historical validation for the biblical patriarchs.</note>; from the Greek

# P R E F A C E.

*writers Alexander Polyhistor, Hecataeus, and Nicolaus of Damascus, who adds to his testimony that the name of Abraham remained famous in Damascus for a long time: to which one may add the Latin writer Justinus, or rather Trogus Pompeius, whom he abridged, mentioning Abraham as King of Damascus: and although some distortion of the truth <note>The author suggests that while these secular accounts confirm Abraham's existence, they often contain legendary or historically inaccurate details, such as naming him a King of Damascus.</note> may lie hidden here, no one should wonder that the fame of my Hero clung to that city for so long; since his victory over the Armenian Princes occurred essentially within its sight. We find even more extensively in the Church Father <note>original: "Outvader," literally "Old Father." This refers to the early Christian bishops and scholars known as the Church Fathers.</note> Eusebius the testimony of the aforementioned Greek writer Polyhistor, praised by that Church Father as a man of great spirit and deep learning; this man, in turn, cited in his History of the Jews the testimonies of Eupolemus, Artapanus, and Melo, who had written an entire book against the Jews: in which testimonies, without transcribing each one at length, one can find the truth confirmed of Abraham's birth in Ur of the Chaldeans; his Migration or Journey at God’s command; his Wisdom in the movement of the heavens <note>original: "Hemelloop." Historical traditions often credited Abraham with teaching astronomy or astrology to the Egyptians and Phoenicians.</note>; the Captivity of Lot,*

and again *Deliverance*, with the encounter of *Melchizedek*, in circumstances sufficiently corresponding with the account of *Moses*; his Journey to *Egypt*, and his experiences there, his two-fold *Marriage*; the Sacrifice of *Isaac*, commanded by God, yet prevented by an Angel, who provided a ram in his place; his Sons from another *Marriage*, and the sending away of the same to *Arabia*. These may be found more extensively in *Eusebius* in his *Evangelical Preparation* <note>original: "Euangelische Voorbereidinge," referring to the *Praeparatio Evangelica*, a 4th-century work by Eusebius of Caesarea intended to prove the superiority of Christianity over pagan religions using their own writings.</note>.

However much these excellent testimonies of so many principal parts of our *History* are here and there tarnished with fabulous stories and circumstances—according to the measure that the light of *Truth* shone, more or less, by twilight or reflection, into the dark intellect of those peoples—and however little these accounts hold up as equal in their details when tested upon the pure touchstone of the holy pages <note>The "holy pages" refers to the Bible; the author uses the metaphor of a "touchstone," a hard stone used to test the purity of gold or silver.</note>, the more beautifully and illustriously must the fine gold—the truth of our history—display its hallmark and shine forth in the eyes of *Unbelief*; especially when it sees the truth of *Moses’* Writings confirmed, as if by so many blind witnesses <note>The term "blind witnesses" refers to non-Christian or "pagan" historians who confirm biblical events without realizing they are supporting the authority of the Hebrew Scriptures.</note>. Should anyone, however, still offer resistance, sucking poison from the same flowers, which

Keep silence before me, O Islands, and let the Peoples renew their strength: let them draw near, then let them speak: let us approach the judgment together.

Who has raised up that Righteous man <insert>*Abraham*</insert> from the east? <note>original: "opgang," literally the rising of the sun, referring to the East.</note> called him to his foot? given the Heathens <note>In this context, "Heathens" refers to the various pagan nations or Gentiles surrounding the early Israelites.</note> before his face, and made him rule over Kings? has given them to his sword as dust, and to his bow as driven stubble?

That he pursued them, and passed through in peace, by a path that he had not gone with his feet? Who has wrought this, etc.: I, the Lord, who am the first, and with the last, I am the same, etc.

# P R E F A C E.

...provide the attentive Reader with sufficient reason to see that we depict the Church rituals <term>Kerkgebaren: religious rites and outward ceremonies of a faith</term> of this false religion merely from our own imagination, like a cloud near the sun, so that the rays of heavenly light from the true religion might fall more powerfully upon the image of my Abraham, to present him in an even more beautiful light. For this reason, no one can or should demand any other proof from me, except that the idolatry of *Isis* and *Osiris* <note>Isis and Osiris were the primary deities of the ancient Egyptian pantheon.</note> can be traced back to that ancient time. Some doubt this, along with certain interpreters of Fables <note>The author uses "Fables" to refer to Greek and Roman mythology.</note> who consider *Iö*, the daughter of *Inachus*, King of *Argos*, to be the Egyptian *Isis*. This proof shall be established for us by those writers and far-famed antiquarians who are listed in our eleventh booklet on page 264.

Moving past this, I do not believe that my depiction of God’s glorious Heavenly Council <term>Hemelraat: the assembly of divine beings or personified attributes surrounding God's throne</term>, also found in the same second book, will lead any sensitive Readers toward a mistaken idea, as a certain Theologian told me he feared; as if the personified representation of every Attribute of the most perfect Supreme Being <term>Opperwezen: a formal title for God, emphasizing His status as the highest entity</term> would instill a concept of divisibility in the Reader, which would contradict His pure Simplicity <note>In historical theology, "Simplicity" (Latin: *simplicitas*) is the doctrine that God is without parts and is not composed of a variety of elements; He is a single, unified essence.</note>...

<meta>catchword: men</meta>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his literary choices, specifically addressing the theological concern that personifying God’s attributes (like Mercy or Justice) might lead readers to believe God is "divisible" rather than a single, simple essence. # PREFACE.

One might say: in this matter, I believe I have taken every possible care; however, against a lack of understanding, one cannot arm oneself:

->According to the capacity of the reader, books have their destiny. <note>original: "Pro captu lectoris habent sua fata libelli." This famous line from the Roman grammarian Terentianus Maurus suggests that a book's success or meaning depends on the reader's own perception.</note><-

But: because we humans, with our finite intellect, can only understand things piecemeal through reasoning <term>Redenkavelinge: the process of logical deduction or breaking down complex ideas into smaller parts to understand them</term> and composition; it has pleased the merciful Godhead <term>Godtheit: the divine essence or being of God</term> Himself to come to the aid of our weak understanding. He presents His infinite, incomprehensible Being—in which, in truth, no composition or division exists—from various perspectives, as if through so many aids to our contemplation. He even presents these specific views in His written Word using physical imagery and symbols <term>Zinnebeelden: allegorical representations or "sense-images" used to explain spiritual truths through physical objects</term> of human limbs, and even human passions. Thus, His sovereign and unchangeable Will is shown through the lens of a princely Council <term>Vorstelyken Raat: a metaphor for God's deliberative process, depicted as a royal court or assembly of divine advisors</term>, including counsels, thoughts, intentions, decrees, and the ways of God. Proofs of this are found everywhere in the holy pages, and especially—which we must not overlook—in the poetic portion <note>The "poetic portion" refers to books of the Bible such as Job, Psalms, and the Prophets, which use heavy metaphor and personification.</note> of the same, ready at hand.

->\* \* \*<-

<summary>The author defends his use of the "Heavenly Council" metaphor by arguing that since the human mind is finite, God uses human-like symbols and "princely" imagery in the Bible to help people understand His infinite nature.</summary>
<keywords>divine nature, finite intellect, metaphors, Heavenly Council, biblical poetry, personification, theological defense</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of his literary choices, specifically addressing the theological concern that personifying God’s attributes (like Mercy or Justice) might lead readers to believe God is "divisible" rather than a single, simple essence. He argues that such metaphors are necessary for the limited human mind.</meta>

# PREFACE.

*and close at hand; as if the Holy Spirit himself granted the Art of Poetry more freedom for this purpose. The theologian and excellent Poet J. Vollenhove <note>Joannes Vollenhove (1631–1708) was a prominent Dutch minister and poet known for his epic religious works.</note> made use of this same freedom in his superb poem, the Triumph of the Cross, in the following verses, in which he follows the trail of the thoughts of the clever Kaspar van Baarle <note>Caspar Barlaeus (1584–1648), a famous Dutch polymath and poet.</note>, in his oration on the manger of Christ:*

> What wonder-filled salvation, what a sight
> My soul perceives upon this stake: <note>The "stake" refers to the Cross on which Christ was crucified.</note>
> Here I find God's wrathful vengeance;
> And here, merciful pardon.
> Justice insisted upon punishment;
> Mercy pleaded for safe passage,
> Here God's Wisdom stepped between them,
> Which gave satisfaction to them both.

*Such a Reader, whom I now imagine having led by the hand across these heights, I now dare to let go alone, and wish him a good journey, not doubting that he will now step more lightly over the lesser poetic liberties; especially when he reminds himself how often*

<summary>The author concludes his defense of using poetic metaphors for God by citing the precedent set by respected poets J. Vollenhove and Caspar Barlaeus. He provides a verse illustrating how God's Wisdom reconciles the conflicting demands of Justice and Mercy at the Cross.</summary>

<keywords>J. Vollenhove, Caspar Barlaeus, Kruistriumf, Poetic Liberty, Justice and Mercy, Redemption, Dutch Golden Age Poetry, Christology</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s defense of poetic license in religious writing. The previous page ended by suggesting that even in the most serious contexts, certain freedoms are necessary, especially when one considers how often these techniques are used in religious discourse.</meta>

# PREFACE.

the sacred pulpit, in explaining the holy Antiquities <note>In this context, "Antiquities" refers to the study of Biblical history and the ancient world of the Old and New Testaments.</note>, must make use of such liberties—whether as the thoughts of the Theologians <term>Godtgeleerden: scholars of divinity</term>, or as original inventions—both to rescue oneself from difficulty and to accommodate the weak listener. This is to say nothing of the many spiritual allusions and mere guesses which often pass there for current coin <note>The author uses "current coin" as a metaphor for ideas that are widely accepted as truth without being strictly proven.</note>. Indeed, we do not doubt that the reader, having reached the end of our Literary Path <term>Letterbane: the course of a written work or a literary career</term>, will through careful contemplation come to this conclusion: that heavenly Truth cannot hold itself offended if the holy Art of Poetry, out of a pure reverence for the light that radiates from her divine bosom, comes to offer her homage, draping her in a proud mantle embroidered with flowers, and placing a crown of bright gemstones upon her head.

One more word to the discerning and poetic Reader, and with that we take our leave; we request him to use our labor with as much benefit and edification as possible, and to that end to lay aside that nagging prejudice <term>vooroordeel</term> by which one immediately rejects everything that is new, as soon as one finds it not precisely consistent with this or that arbitrary law, put forward by foreigners <note>This likely refers to the strict rules of French Neoclassicism, which many Dutch poets of the time felt were too restrictive for religious epics.</note>,

<summary>The author argues that just as preachers use metaphors to help their congregations understand the divine, poetry serves to honor Truth by dressing it in beautiful language. He concludes by asking the reader to judge his work on its own merits rather than by rigid, foreign literary rules.</summary>

<keywords>pulpit rhetoric, Biblical Antiquities, Theologians, poetic license, personification of Truth, Literary Path, Neoclassical rules, Dutch literature</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s preface, specifically concluding a defense against the rigid imposition of foreign (likely French Neoclassical) rules on Dutch poetry. It transitions from a critique of restrictive "laws" to a justification for the naturalistic and varied tone of the work.</meta>

# P R E F A C E.

*...scribed, and placed our free Art as a burdensome yoke upon the neck. I have not wished to bend the truth to foreign laws, or to manipulate it to my own hand, as a Painter does his Lay-figure <term>Leeman: a jointed wooden mannequin used by artists to study poses and proportions</term>; but I have preserved the same in its natural state, and left my History <note>In this context, "History" refers to the narrative or epic poem itself.</note>, into which we enter almost in the middle <note>A reference to the classical technique of "in media res," where a story begins in the midst of the action.</note>, to its own course. If my thoughts are sometimes found lowly, let one consider that my Hero is a Shepherd, and that the tone must also rise and fall according to the demands of the subject matter: but where the power of my spirit or art falters, let one take it for the best, and consider that a good tree produces leaves as well as flowers and fruits, under which the same hang safely, as if in a lovely shade, and ripen most fragrantly.*

<image-desc>A decorative hand-colored woodcut tailpiece featuring symmetrical scrollwork, floral motifs, and a central shield-like element in shades of red, blue, and gold.</image-desc>

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, the author defends his use of a naturalistic style and the "in media res" structure, arguing that the tone of the poem must match the humble status of his protagonist, a shepherd.</summary>
<keywords>poetic theory, artistic freedom, lay-figure, pastoral hero, Dutch literature, in media res, metaphor of the tree</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page serves as a formal dedication to Arnold Hoogvliet, the author of the epic poem "Abraham de Aartsvader" (Abraham the Patriarch), published in 1728. It follows the author's own preface and transitions into a series of laudatory poems written by Hoogvliet’s peers. This specific poem is written in Latin elegiac couplets, a classical meter often used for formal greetings, and reimagines the biblical call of Abraham through the lens of Roman epic poetry.</meta>

### TO
## THE MOST INGENIOUS POET
# ARNOLD HOOGVLIET,
### ON THE OCCASION OF HIS BRINGING TO LIGHT
### THE LIFE OF ABRAHAM
### *IN DUTCH VERSE.*

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'C' featuring a floral or foliate motif within a square frame.</image-desc>FROM the Chaldean people, the father <note>original: "pater"; referring to Abraham</note> is ordered to depart—the son of Terah <term>Tharaïdes: a classical-style patronymic meaning "son of Terah," Abraham’s father</term>—by the highest God, who rules the stars.
For indeed, the ruler of blue Olympus <note>A classical poetic name for Heaven, used here to give the biblical story the weight of a Greek or Roman epic</note> spoke:
"Alas, flee from that condemned flock! You shall be ours.
O you, who are to be called the father of as many children as there are stars between the hidden and the visible poles! <note>A reference to the entire span of the heavens, from the North to the South celestial poles</note>
I shall be a faithful guide through the inhospitable wastes of your journey.
Do not fear! I am your shield and your reward.
A covenant of friendship I shall strike with you through all the ages,
to be made joyful with blessings yet to come."

<meta>This page continues the celebratory introduction to Arnold Hoogvliet’s epic poem "Abraham de Aartsvader." Promising thus through His grace
To bless all the earth through his offspring.
Where he, driven by famine, leaves the climate
Of Canaan, and flees to Egypt:
Which immediately plunges him into grief through the lust
Of Pharaoh, who has his wife abducted;
Although that Prince pays for this with a thousand calamities,
Until he reluctantly releases her.
Where he so proudly girds weapons to his loins,
And advances into the field with his troops
To seek revenge for his brother Lot <note>Though his nephew, Lot is often referred to as "brother" in accordance with biblical and archaic usage</note> from so many a warrior
Who held him and all his goods in chains.

Returns his Sarah to him untouched,
After God made known to him in a distressing dream
The source of his land’s misery:
Whereupon the curse of heaven, which this evil
Had laid upon Gerar’s neck, vanishes like a mist,
As soon as the hero <note>original: "helt"; a common way 18th-century poets referred to biblical patriarchs to give them epic status</note> casts himself down
In prayer before the Lord of Lords.
Where he, like a rock amidst the waves,
Courageously awaits the most violent dangers,

Knows nothing of hesitating or yielding;
But, patient, and ready at God’s command,

Whatever harsh storm may strike his slender keel,
And, laying down calmly upon the woodpile,
His Isaac, his son, his heart’s delight,
His only heir—that sprout, for whose sake
God promised him a lineage of Princes,
Whom he saw rise from Sarah in her old age,
In a manner that amazed Nature herself—
He grasps the blade; and just as he is ready for the offering,
That same God restrains him, and turns the edge of the knife.
Where Hebron sees him weeping over the remains of his spouse, <note>original: "gâ"; an archaic Dutch term for a life partner or spouse.</note>

The earthly remains of his dear Sarah.
Where he summons Rebekah from the land of Nahor,
So that her hand might be given to Isaac.
Where Keturah warms his second bed with her charms,
To father son after son from that union,
Until at last he falls, weakened by death;
Where his Isaac, with such great sorrow...

Closes the eyes, granting him, alongside

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How laudably **HOOGVLIET’S** swan-quill <note>The "swan-quill" (Dutch: *zwanenschacht*) is a poetic metaphor for a refined writing instrument, suggesting the poet sings as beautifully as a swan.</note> acquits itself 
In a subject so worthy of praise! How softly 
Flows here a stream of heavenly honeycombs! 
Which never satiate, but always leave one longing for more. 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Whether he depicts God, burning with wrath, 
As He broadly convenes the heavenly tribunal, <term>heavenly tribunal: original "hemelvierschaer," referring to the divine court of justice, traditionally depicted as a square of benches.</term>

Whether he describes idolatry running rampant

And helps celebrate the feast of Isis with the Egyptians, 
With those solemn gestures, 
Carrying around the dog-headed Anubis, 
And loudly lamenting the death of Osiris. <note>Hoogvliet includes these descriptions of Egyptian mythology to contrast Abraham's monotheism with the pagan environment of his travels.</note> 
Whether he leads the innocent boy <note>Referring to Isaac.</note> toward 
The hill, and makes him pile up the wood 
For the sacrificial altar, while the sorrowful father 
Then feels his blood freeze in his veins from terror,

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the praise of Arnold Hoogvliet’s epic poem "Abraham de Aartsvader." The previous page described the dramatic tension of the binding of Isaac; this page concludes that scene and shifts to a personified "Poetry" crowning the author for choosing sacred themes over pagan ones.</meta>

Then he takes courage again, before he, comforted in God,
Sets the knife to the throat of that fruit of marriage. <note>Referring to Isaac, the "fruit" of Abraham and Sarah's union.</note>
The Art of Poetry, whom Ignorance always
Dares to assault too shamelessly with unwashed hands, <note>A classical metaphor: "unwashed hands" refers to those who are profane or uninitiated attempting to handle something sacred or sophisticated.</note>
Blossoms, like a morning rose, with joy
At the sight of this scene, and cries out happily:
Now let **Apollo** <note>The Greek god of music and poetry; the poet argues that Christian subjects surpass classical ones.</note> yield, with all that poets
Dream of there, possessed by their wild spirits.
In this music, of such pure tone,
No **Clio** <term>Clio: the Greek Muse of history and epic poetry, traditionally credited with inspiring great writers.</term> beats the time; but a **Cherub**.
No **Hippocrene** <term>Hippocrene: (original: "Hengstebron") a spring on Mount Helicon created by the hoof of the winged horse Pegasus, said to bring poetic inspiration.</term> causes that clattering here;
But the **Jordan** rustles here with holy water.
Here no two peaks of **Parnassus** rise; <note>Mount Parnassus was the home of the Muses in Greek mythology.</note>
But the mountains of **Moriah**, where, at that moment,
God Himself was present, and stayed Abraham's strike;
At the end of this steep path,
No laurels raise themselves, but the oak leaves of **Mamre**; <note>The "Oaks of Mamre" refer to the place where Abraham pitched his tent and built an altar to God.</note>
A foliage, compared to which the luster of those laurels is dull;
A foliage, from which we rightly weave a wreath,
To place it ourselves on **HOOGVLIET’S** head.

->K. BOON VAN ENGELANT.<-

-> ON <-

# A B R A H A M,
-> THE <-
### P A T R I A R C H; <note>original: "Aertsvader"</note>
-> B Y <-
### ARNOLD HOOGVLIET.

Homer’s Hero, however great and proud,
Must now yield, along with Maro’s <note>Maro: refers to the Roman poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro), author of the *Aeneid*.</note> Commander:
   Let the Swan of the Vecht <note>A poetic nickname for a Dutch poet living near the river Vecht, likely referring to Jan Baptist Wellekens or similar contemporaries who wrote in the classical tradition.</note> be silent, though the elegance of Pindus <note>Mount Pindus: a mountain in Greece sacred to the Muses; "Pindus' flourish" refers to the high, classical style of poetry.</note>
Plays through his lofty epic poems:
   For HOOGVLIET sings of a greater Hero,
   And Prince, of many princely things;
   His Warrior grows great here in the field,
   And in contemplations of faith;
   Yes, by the God of gods, fitly
   Declared the heir of the world.

<meta>This page continues the introductory commendations for Arnold Hoogvliet’s epic poem. While the previous page established Abraham's superiority over classical Greek and Roman heroes like Achilles and Aeneas, this page shifts to addressing the audience—both the historical Israelites and the "spiritual Israel" (the Christian readers)—inviting them to find joy and spiritual comfort in this poetic retelling of the Patriarch's life.</meta>

<sig>* * 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: Daer</meta>

The great Patriarch Abraham,
Losing himself in God’s ways;
In the sanctuary by the holy Lamb,
Lives again in Dutch <note>original: "duitſche" — In the 18th century, the term "Duits" or "Nederduits" was frequently used to refer to the Dutch language rather than modern German.</note> heroic songs,
To the joy of his countless offspring,
Who boast of their famous Father.
Sublime Poetic work, source of comfort,
Flowed from the inexhaustible vein
Of the best Biblical poetry,
How you delight Zion’s Citizens! <note>Zion’s Citizens: A metaphorical reference to the community of believers or the faithful.</note>

O Ancient, O Princely Priesthood,
That used to dwell according to your tribes,
Where milk and honey flowed everywhere,
To crown you with salvation and favor:
And you, O spiritual Israel,
Circumcised not in the flesh, but in the heart,
After the Man, God’s Companion, <note>God's Companion: Likely a reference to Christ, whom the poet links to the fulfillment of the Abrahamic promise.</note>
Had suffered in the flesh, according to God’s counsel;
O Abraham’s seed, so dearly beloved,
Delight yourselves in this word-print! <note>original: "woordenprint" — A poetic term suggesting a vivid literary portrait or an illustration made through language.</note>

<meta>This page continues the poetic introduction to Arnold Hoogvliet's epic "Abraham the Patriarch." The previous page concluded with Abraham and Lot's decision to separate to avoid conflict between their herdsmen. This page picks up with a description of the land Lot chose and Abraham’s subsequent rescue of Lot after he was captured in war. The catchword at the bottom of the previous page was "Jor-," which leads into "Jordaanstroom" (Jordan River) here. The catchword at the bottom of this page is "'s Mans," likely leading into a discussion of Abraham's character or "The Man's" virtues.</meta>

Jordan River, ancient river of the land,
Which with your green clover-banks,
And winding course and grace
Charmed the wandering eye of Lot, <note>In the biblical narrative (Genesis 13), Lot chose the fertile Jordan Valley because it was well-watered and beautiful, resembling the "garden of the Lord."</note>
You take part in this separation,
And come to mildly water your region,
Now that the field-echoing music of flutes
Combs your waves, and makes them flow smoothly;
While the cattle, in the fresh season,
Frolic more freely in the green.

---

The freedom-depriving fate of war,
Which Lot, by God’s decree, had to meet, <note>Referring to the "War of the Kings" (Genesis 14), in which Lot was captured during a raid on Sodom by an alliance of kings led by Chedorlaomer.</note>
Shall Abraham—who fights with God’s aid—
Exalt to the celestial vault of heroes. <note>original: "heldenhemeltrans" — A poetic 18th-century term suggesting a heavenly gallery or sphere reserved for heroic souls.</note>
How bravely the pious Hero <note>original: "vroome Helt" — Hoogvliet characterizes Abraham as a "pious hero," blending the virtues of a biblical patriarch with the grand style of classical epic poetry.</note>
Girds himself for the rescue of his kinsman! <note>original: "Broeder" — Though Lot was Abraham's nephew, the Dutch "broeder" (brother) was commonly used for close male relatives or members of the same covenant family.</note>
He strikes the enemy; he holds the field:
He is cheered as Lot’s protector,
While he, in his triumph, <note>original: "zegepraal"</note>
Enjoys a royal reception. <note>This refers to the meeting with Melchizedek, the King of Salem and priest of God Most High, who greeted the victorious Abraham with bread and wine.</note>

<meta>This page continues the poetic introduction to Arnold Hoogvliet’s epic "Abraham the Patriarch." The catchword from the previous page, "'s Mans" (The Man's), begins the first line of this page, shifting the focus to Abraham’s supreme test of faith: the binding of Isaac.</meta>

The Man’s great and unshakable hope,
Which God’s covenant promises support,
Led him, tested throughout his life’s course,
To offer up his darling son to God; <note>original: "troetelzoon" — Literally "pet son" or "darling son," a term of deep endearment emphasizing Isaac's status as the beloved, long-awaited heir.</note>
Although he was the first of the innumerable seed
That would give rise to many nations,
His Covenant-God, and his refuge,
Could bring that son back to life from death:
See then, by way of a likeness, <note>original: "by gelykenis" — This refers to biblical typology, where the near-sacrifice and "return" of Isaac is viewed as a prophetic symbol or "likeness" of the resurrection of Christ.</note>
How Isaac has risen.

God’s Temple Mount, renowned for this, <note>Mount Moriah, traditionally identified as the site of the Binding of Isaac and the future location of the Temple in Jerusalem.</note>
Can bear witness to this sacrificial deed:
That mountain, whose peak soars toward heaven,
Has seen the world’s Heir bow down <note>original: "'s waerelts Erfgenaem" — Referring to Abraham, who through his faith was promised that his descendants would inherit the earth.</note>
Before the trial-command, a stern decree,
Readily performed through the Man’s will.
Let him who desires come and hear how clearly
My Poet has raised his voice here,
As he gracefully plays and dwells
Upon Abraham’s godliness. <note>original: "Godtvruchtigheit" — A core virtue in 18th-century Dutch literature, signifying a blend of deep piety, fear of God, and moral integrity.</note>

<meta>This page continues the poetic introduction to Arnold Hoogvliet’s 1728 epic, "Abraham de Aartsvader" (Abraham the Patriarch). Following the description of the sacrifice of Isaac on the previous page, the poet now shifts focus to the legacy of Abraham's faith and addresses his descendants. The catchword 't Gaat (It goes/proceeds) at the bottom of the page indicates the transition to the first book of the epic on the following page.</meta>

What do I recount and sing in this song
Of the Hero's <note>Abraham</note> illustrious deeds,
Which the ancestral people <note>the Jewish people</note> joyfully hear and see
Proclaimed loudly, and described as they wish;
While the exalted poetic skill
Keeps the Daughter of Zion <note>original: "Siöns Dochter" — A biblical personification of Jerusalem or the people of Israel.</note> enraptured,
As the ancient power and loveliness
Play through this Divine poetic gift,
In which Jacob’s offspring <note>original: "Jakobs kroost"</note> lose themselves,
Those who choose Abraham’s path of life. <note>original: "Abrams wandelweg" — Literally "Abraham's walking-way," referring to his journey of faith and obedience to God.</note>

But you, O ancient lineage of Jacob!
Strangers scattered far and wide,
Who have despised God's Anointed One <note>original: "Godts Gezalfden" — Referring to the Messiah. In Hoogvliet's 18th-century Christian context, this is an appeal for the Jewish people to recognize Jesus as the fulfillment of the covenant with Abraham.</note>
By plotting against his life, <note>original: "naar zyn' blanken hals te dingen" — Literally "to bargain for his white neck," a poetic Dutch idiom for seeking someone's death or execution.</note>
Misled by your own false delusions!
Learn, learn here in these final days
To set out upon your father’s paths:
Turn yourselves, as is God’s good pleasure,
Toward your King, the son of David,
Kiss him; swear your homage before his throne.

->\* \* \*<-
->\* \*<-

<vocab>Hero, Daughter of Zion, Jacob’s offspring, Abraham’s path of life, God’s Anointed One, David’s son</vocab>

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, the poet celebrates the beauty of Abraham's faith and issues a direct, proselytizing appeal to the Jewish diaspora to embrace the "son of David" as their King. This reflects the common 18th-century literary theme of linking Old Testament history with New Testament theology.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham, Daughter of Zion, Jacob's offspring, Jacob's lineage, Messiah, David's son, Jewish diaspora, Arnold Hoogvliet, 18th-century poetry</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory commendatory verses for Arnold Hoogvliet’s epic poem "Abraham de Aartsvader" (1728). The previous page ended with an appeal to the Jewish people to recognize the Christian Messiah. This page, written by the poet Petrus Schim, celebrates the power of Hoogvliet's poetry to spiritually "reborn" the audience and expand the reach of the church, concluding with a call to crown the poet with laurels for his heroic literary achievement.</meta>

It goes well, the Maiden Israel <note>A personification of the Jewish people, often used in biblical and poetic contexts.</note>
Lends to my friend her poetry-loving ears,
Yes, she is from God’s well of grace
Reborn through his poetry,
To which the Dutch mount of song <note>original: "Neêrlands zangberg" — A reference to Parnassus, the mythological home of the Muses, here representing the flourishing state of Dutch literature.</note> contributes.
Thus God’s kingdom of salvation expands its borders,
As far as the art of poetry pleases everyone;
[To reach] the nations who wander from God’s path of salvation;
As far as the sun and silver moon
Go in a choral dance <note>original: "ten reie gaan" — A "rei" refers to a traditional structured dance or a choir in a play; here it suggests the cosmic, rhythmic order of the heavens.</note> praising God.

---

Thus shall this Poetic Work, according to my wish,
See God's church expand its grapevine
From sea to sea, from border to border,
Where its tendrils spread out,
Full of fruits of Righteousness,
To praise God’s doctrine of grace.
O Zion, have you woven a wreath
Which you wish to award to my Poet?
Come, place it upon his brow, come now,
This Heroic Work <note>original: "Heldenwerk" — A term specifically used for an epic poem, which was considered the highest and most difficult form of "heroic" literary endeavor.</note> demands laurel leaves.

->P. SCHIM. <note>Petrus Schim (1687–1755), a Dutch minister and poet who was a contemporary and friend of Hoogvliet.</note><-

<summary>This concluding commendatory poem by Petrus Schim praises Arnold Hoogvliet's epic for its spiritual impact, suggesting it has the power to bring the Jewish people to grace and expand the influence of the church. It ends by calling for Hoogvliet to be honored with a laurel wreath for his "heroic" literary contribution.</summary>

# ON
# ARNOLD HOOGVLIET’S
## A B R A H A M,
### T H E
### *P A T R I A R C H.* <note>original: "Aertsvader" — Specifically referring to the biblical patriarchs, the founding fathers of the Israelites.</note>

God’s Friend, the Patriarch Abraham,
The son of Terah, from the tribe
Sprung from Shem, receives new life,
Through **Hoogvliet's** artful poetic brush; <note>original: "dichtpenseel" — A common 18th-century metaphor (Ut Pictura Poesis) comparing the poet to a painter who "paints" scenes for the reader using words instead of pigments.</note>
His hand, driven by a higher Hand, <note>An allusion to divine inspiration, suggesting God guided the poet's writing.</note>
Paints the man's deeds in a tableau,
With fitting paint and strokes,
Which through an age of ages shall not
Fade nor grow pale,
Come, discerning eyes, look, and see. <note>An invitation to the sophisticated reader to appreciate the quality and spiritual depth of the work.</note>

<summary>This page features a dedication poem that introduces Arnold Hoogvliet’s epic "Abraham," characterizing the poet as a divinely-inspired artist who has immortalized the life of the biblical patriarch through his "poetic brush."</summary>
<keywords>Arnold Hoogvliet, Abraham, Patriarch, Terah, Shem, divine inspiration, word-painting, Dutch literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory tributes to Arnold Hoogvliet. While the previous page focused on the visual "painting" of the biblical narrative, this poem by Jacob Schim emphasizes the poet’s literary lineage, comparing Hoogvliet to Moses and asserting that his work surpasses the "fables" of classical antiquity.</meta>

You shall, with Abraham, great in name,
O **HOOGVLIET**, soar through Fame,
On every tongue, through every age.
You live, through elegant biblical verse, <note>original: "bybeldicht" — Poetry specifically based on stories from the Bible, a prestigious genre in the 18th century.</note>
Like Moses, the Leader <note>original: "d'Hertogh" — Literally "The Duke," used here in the archaic sense of a sovereign leader or commander of the Israelites.</note> of the Hebrews,
Through his sacred chronicle. Now yield
The fables of the ancient poets. <note>A common rhetorical device in Christian epics, claiming that biblical history is more truthful and glorious than Greek or Roman mythology.</note>
You follow, O **HOOGVLIET**, the holy trail
Of God’s consecrated founders of the Song-Mountain, <note>original: "Zangbergstichters" — A reference to the Muses of Mount Parnassus, here Christianized to refer to divinely inspired biblical poets like King David.</note>
Thus building Zion’s Temple choir.

---

Your subject matter, fitting style,
And language, polished with **Vondel's** file, <note>original: "Vondels vyl" — Joost van den Vondel was the most famous Dutch poet of the 17th century. To say one's language was "polished with Vondel’s file" was the highest possible compliment for Dutch literary craft.</note>
Charm and edify poetic ears.
The Bride of Jesus, <note>A traditional theological metaphor for the Christian Church.</note> the spiritual seed
Born from father Abraham,
Processes, in priestly robes,
In a chorus to your biblical echoes.
May your brow be crowned, Church Poet,
With laurel leaves and fresh palms.
Gain more reward through diligence and sweat.

->JACOB SCHIM.<-

Since you, time and again, seized by pure delight,
->The flowers of your poetry<-
->Around my eloquent painting,<- <note>original: "schildery" — A common 18th-century literary metaphor where a poem is compared to a visual painting (the concept of "ut pictura poesis"). The author is referring to a work of his own that Hoogvliet had previously honored with a poem.</note>

<meta>This page continues the celebratory poem by Jacob Schim addressed to Arnold Hoogvliet. In the previous section, Schim expressed his gratitude for Hoogvliet’s friendship; here, he shifts his focus to the subject of Hoogvliet's work: the biblical patriarch Abraham.</meta>

I feel a noble passion surging through all my veins,
->To weave a wreath of my wit upon this paper<-
->For your God-consecrated Man,<- <note>Refers to Abraham, the subject of Hoogvliet’s epic poem.</note>

->Who built temples of unity<-
->Within his breast, wherein he sought to add<-

->From the claws of the enemy,<- <note>Refers to the narrative in Genesis 14 where Abraham rescues his nephew Lot from capturing kings.</note>

To whom, by Melchizedek, the prince of peace and life,
->And of eternal righteousness,<-
->God's favor and salvation were promised,<-
Which he alone could give to him in full strength;

<meta>This page continues Jacob Schim’s laudatory poem for Arnold Hoogvliet. While the previous page focused on the biblical narrative of Abraham’s life and his ultimate devotion to God, this section shifts to praise Hoogvliet himself and his hometown, Vlaardingen. It uses the classical trope of personifying a local river (the Meuse) to celebrate the poet’s achievement.</meta>

Who ever saw the nimble Meuse <note>original: "Maes" — The river Meuse, which flows past Vlaardingen. In classical poetry, rivers were often personified as deities or nymphs.</note> rise more quickly and readily
->From her water jar,<-
->Combing out her green tresses,<- <note>original: "pruik" — literally "wig," but here referring to the water plants or reeds that form the river nymph's hair.</note>
Than now, O HOOGVLIET, to kiss your bountifully gifted pen,
And to drink in with her ear the echo
->Of your refined biblical songs;<-
->To hang up your tableaus,<-
Full of vivid imagery, in her silver water-choir; <note>A "water-choir" refers to a gathering of water nymphs or the musical sound of the flowing river.</note>
So that she may say to everyone who comes to sail her streams:
->“This charming image<-
->Of Abraham, which delights every eye,<-
Has HOOGVLIET thus fashioned through the sounding of his strings?” <note>Comparing the poet’s work to playing a lyre or harp, a standard metaphor for epic poetry.</note>
*O Vlaardingen! <note>original: "Vlaerding" — The Dutch city where Arnold Hoogvliet lived and worked.</note> small remnant of a much greater city,*
->Long ago drowned or scattered,<-
->With your illustrious Countly Courts,<- <note>original: "Gravenhoven" — Vlaardingen was a significant administrative center for the early Counts of Holland in the 11th century before being eclipsed by other cities.</note>
Now thrust your gray crown toward heaven out of the waters
Of the bright Meuse, and rise much higher than your neighbors,
->Now that your gifted fellow citizen!<-
->Through his genius, enlarges your name,<-
And surrounds you through his song with firm walls
Of holiness and virtue, which make cities endure:
->Arise! Arise! Exalt these heroic songs!<-
->Why do you let your head hang so low?<-
The Deity accepts no glimmer of deep sighs:
No cowardly, whining talk nor withdrawn appearance,
->No eyes wet with the dew of tears,<-
->Often mirrors of feigned repentance.<-
Arise! Let joy at last be read upon your countenance.

<summary>In this concluding section of his poem, Jacob Schim personifies the river Meuse as a nymph praising Arnold Hoogvliet’s epic "Abraham," and he calls upon the ancient town of Vlaardingen to take pride in its famous resident poet.</summary>

It is a wedding: Christ is already married to His Bride. <note>original: "Bruit" — In Christian theology, the "Bride of Christ" refers to the Church or the collective body of believers.</note>

# A B R A H A M,

If the noble Art of Poetry <note>original: "Dichtkunst"</note> was ever lauded and highly praised,
->Shown an eternal honor,<-

## THE PATRIARCH,

When <term>Hoogvliet|Arnold Hoogvliet (1687–1763) was a highly successful Dutch poet whose epic on the life of Abraham became one of the most widely read books of the 18th century.</term>’s beautiful Muse <note>original: "Dichtheldin" — literally "poetic heroine," a personification of the poet's inspiration or the spirit of the poem itself.</note>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory poem by Dirk Smits, celebrating the publication of the fourth edition of Arnold Hoogvliet’s "Abraham the Patriarch." The previous page described the Muse watching Abraham ascend to heaven; here, she rises with him, adorning him with heavenly symbols before presenting herself once more to the public in this new edition.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>3.</sig>

Soon she rises, singing, from the earth;
She follows the great Hero <note>The "Hero" refers to the biblical patriarch Abraham, the subject of the epic poem.</note> in his exalted journey,
Crafting for him a crown of pearls, diamonds,
And eternally-shining sun-gold;
She adorns his white garment with heavenly amaranths <term>amaranths|In classical poetry, the amaranth is a legendary flower that never fades, symbolizing immortality and the eternal fame of the subject.</term>
And flowers of Paradise, whose splendor never ages.

<image-desc>A small decorative floral ornament consisting of three stylized flowers or stars.</image-desc>

Thus, along a trail of glowing radiance,
Like a new sun, three times over in her orbit,
With an inimitable course, escaped from all her burdens,
She raised herself, at last, so high,
That those who watched her, on her illustrious paths,
Lost sight of her, despite the song-yearning heart <note>original: "zangziek hart" — literally "song-sick," describing a heart so filled with the desire for poetry and music that it feels a physical ache.</note>.

<image-desc>A small decorative floral ornament consisting of three stylized flowers or stars.</image-desc>

Therefore she now clothes herself, before every eye,
For the fourth time <note>A reference to this being the fourth edition of the book.</note>, in newly reborn light,
More beautiful than she ever descended before the art-loving eye.
O friends of Virtue and Poetry! Gaze,
Gaze eagerly upon her brilliance!... No sun shoots purer rays!...
Take heed, before she escapes you!... I lose myself in her flight!

->D. SMITS.<- <note>Dirk Smits (1702–1752) was a prominent Dutch poet from Rotterdam, a contemporary of Hoogvliet, known for his lyrical and pastoral works.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the laudatory poem by Dirk Smits, using the metaphor of a rising sun and heavenly ascension to celebrate the fourth edition of Hoogvliet’s work. The poem exhorts lovers of art and virtue to appreciate the "reborn light" of this new printing.</summary>

<keywords>Dirk Smits, Arnold Hoogvliet, Abraham the Patriarch, fourth edition, Muse, immortality, Dutch Golden Age literature, amaranth</keywords>

<meta>This page follows a poem by Dirk Smits in the front matter of the book. It features a new commendatory sonnet by Willem vander Pot, a contemporary poet, celebrating the new edition of Arnold Hoogvliet’s "Abraham the Patriarch." While the previous page focused on the Muse's ascent, this poem compares Hoogvliet’s Christian subject matter to the classical epics of antiquity, specifically Virgil’s Aeneid.</meta>

->ON THE NEW PRINTING<-

->O F<-

# A B R A H A M,
## THE PATRIARCH,
->B Y<-
## ARNOLD HOOGVLIET.

Whoever takes delight in the sublime song of Poetry,
Whose soul could be enraptured by Maro’s <note>original: "Maroos" — Referring to the Roman poet Publius Vergilius Maro, better known as Virgil, the author of the *Aeneid*.</note> heroic work,
See great ABRAHAM, as the Sun of all Heroes,
Dawning with new luster along HOOGVLIET’S literary meadow.

This Christian Hero of Song—who won the laurel crown
Neither through wild gusts of rhyming,
Nor by brain-disturbing water from Aganippe’s spring <term>Aganippe|In Greek mythology, a spring on Mount Helicon sacred to the Muses, said to inspire those who drank from it. The poet here contrasts pagan inspiration with Christian grace.</term>,
But through God’s Pentecostal fire—
May wear such a wreath of honor to the glory of Heaven.

O Poets, who honor only the art of mythology <note>original: "Fabeldichtkunst" — literally "the art of fable-poetry," referring to classical Greek and Roman myths which were seen as "fables" compared to Biblical truth.</note>,
And inappropriately still decorate your work with its floral ornaments,
Learn here, along a nobler path, to strive directly toward the stars.

Let VIRGIL live, with his Hero <note>A reference to Aeneas, the protagonist of Virgil's *Aeneid*.</note>, immortalized on this earth;
But you, O HOOGVLIET! You, worthy of an even higher artistic reward,
Shall live, with your ABRAHAM, even in Heaven!

->*WILLEM VANDER POT.*<-

<summary>A dedicatory sonnet by Willem vander Pot praising Arnold Hoogvliet's epic poem. The author argues that Hoogvliet’s Christian subject matter and divine inspiration make his work superior to the classical pagan epics of Virgil.</summary>
<keywords>Arnold Hoogvliet, Willem vander Pot, Abraham the Patriarch, Virgil, Aganippe, Christian poetry, epic poetry, Pentecostal fire</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of commendatory poems included in the front matter of the fourth edition of Arnold Hoogvliet’s "Abraham the Patriarch." While the previous poem by Willem vander Pot focused on comparing Hoogvliet’s work to classical epics like Virgil’s Aeneid, this sonnet by Nikolaes Versteeg emphasizes the divine origin of the poet’s inspiration and the eternal, spiritual reward awaiting the author.</meta>

->ON THE FOURTH EDITION<-

->O F<-

# A B R A H A M,
### *THE PATRIARCH,*

->B Y<-

## ARNOLD HOOGVLIET.

The Heavenly Impulse, which sets the Spirit of Poetry <note>original: "Dichtgeest" — the personified genius or inspiration of the poet.</note> in motion,
Wishing once to show a proof of her powers,
Descended into the laurelled head of HOOGVLIET; she spurs on thoughts,
Conceptions, soul, and sense, with double zeal:

His Spirit of Poetry, thus ignited, rises above sun and moon,
Into the Palace of Heaven, on Cherubic wings <term>Cherubynenschachten|Literally "Cherub-shafts," referring to the wings of the Cherubim, the celestial beings who guard the presence of God.</term>;
Learns Angelic tones; sings of the Father of the generations,
The great ABRAHAM, and his sublime deeds.

Piety <note>original: "De Godsvrucht" — here personified as a figure who welcomes the poem into her temple.</note> had no sooner received this masterpiece
Than she placed it in the middle of her spacious Temple Hall.
Everyone hung upon the divine work with double attention.

O Poet-friend, ARNOLD, who for the fourth time,
Gifts your work to the world, your reward lies not in songs of praise!
Your Hero <note>Referring to the biblical Abraham, the subject of the epic.</note> awaits you with an eternal festive welcome!

->NIKOLAES VERSTEEG.<-

<summary>A commendatory sonnet by Nikolaes Versteeg celebrating the fourth edition of Hoogvliet’s epic poem, portraying the work as divinely inspired and worthy of eternal reward.</summary>
<keywords>Arnold Hoogvliet, Abraham, Nikolaes Versteeg, sonnet, divine inspiration, Piety, fourth edition, Dutch literature</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page serves as the frontispiece and opening invocation of the first book of "Abraham the Patriarch." It introduces the setting in Egypt and the poet's shift from secular/classical themes to sacred history.</meta>

# ABRAHAM,
### THE
## PATRIARCH.
### THE FIRST BOOK.
### *C O N T E N T S.*

> The Patriarch Abram, driven by the pressure of famine,
> Comes to graze his hungry flock in Egyptian pastures;
> There Pharaoh's princes, enchanted by the beautiful face
> Of chaste Sarah, flatter the ear of their King,
> Who, thus ignited with love, snatches her from her sleeping-tent,
> And leads her to the palace in Memphis <note>An ancient capital of Egypt</note> among courtly women.
> He gathers the Council and makes a firm resolution
> To marry the Shepherdess after the festival of Isis <note>The Egyptian goddess of nature and magic</note>;
> While Abram, awaiting help from Heaven,
> Is presented with gifts, and already regarded as Pharaoh’s brother.

---

<margin>The poet’s purpose and Introduction.</margin>
It pleased me in days past to sing in Dutch <note>original: "Nederduitsche"; historically referring to the Low German or Dutch language</note> verses,
Of the Roman gods, and the history of their feasts and years,
Following in the tracks of Sulmo’s literary hero <note>A reference to the Roman poet Ovid, who was born in Sulmo and wrote the *Fasti*, a poem about the Roman calendar and festivals</note>;
But now, now I paint (as my own poetic vein <note>original: "zangaâr," literally "song-vein"</note> swells
With nobler passions, driven by a heavenly fire)
The great Father of the nations <note>Abraham</note> true to life:

<summary>This page provides the "Argument" or plot summary of the first book of the epic, detailing Abraham and Sarah's arrival in Egypt. It concludes with the poet's formal invocation, signaling a transition from writing about Roman mythology to the life of the biblical patriarch Abraham.</summary>

<keywords>Abraham, Sarah, Pharaoh, Egypt, Memphis, Ovid, Dutch literature, Biblical epic, Isis, patriarch</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the poet's invocation. Following the summary of the plot on the previous page, the author now formally asks for divine and scholarly guidance to tell Abraham's story, while preemptively defending his work against skeptics.</meta>

# ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.
<meta>catchword: Depart,</meta>

Yes, it delights me to follow Abram on his journey to Canaan,
And to Egypt, and wherever that divine man
Wanders at the high Command, following him with my strings; <note>The poet refers to his "strings," meaning his lyre or the musical nature of his poetry.</note>
To reveal his wondrous communion with the Godhead;
And, in contemplation of his faith, hope,
And pure godliness, to soar through the whole course of his life
Until he, through God’s eternal grace,
Beholding the great Prince of Peace <note>A traditional Christian interpretation that Abraham "saw" the coming of Christ (the Prince of Peace) through his future descendants.</note> in his lineage,
Descends to the dark grave in his old age.

<margin>Address to the Theological Writers.</margin>
Come now, pious host of holy writers, come
Strengthen my weak spirit in this noble undertaking,
Learned men, teach me to write Biblical truth!
O industrious bees, who, from your youth onwards,
Have wandered through the botanical gardens of the sacred pages
To seek out the honey;
Forgive me if my spirit, wandering through the books,
Should greedily feed upon your delicacies,
That precious stock for my Biblical poetry:
If I draw honey from your full honeycombs;
Forgive me, I shall leave it there again for your benefit:
And perhaps that sweetness, with new fragrance and style,
Will lure you back to the field of my paper.

<margin>He sets unbelief aside.</margin>
But you, the Lost, you, O wanton minds,
O sharp wits, who dare to assault God’s truth,
And mock holy poetry, as if its subject were too lowly,
And its scriptural food <note>original: "letterspys," literally "letter-food," referring to the substance of the written Word.</note> too bland for your strong stomachs;

<summary>The poet expresses his intent to chronicle Abraham's entire life of faith, from his travels to his death. He invokes the "holy writers" (theologians and scholars) for inspiration, comparing their scholarly work to honey that he hopes to refine into poetry, while rebuking those who mock religious literature.</summary>

<keywords>Abraham, Canaan, Prince of Peace, theological writers, biblical poetry, skepticism, faith</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the poet’s prologue. After asking the scholars and writers for their guidance on the previous page, the author now addresses his intended audience—the faithful—while invoking the help of God (the "Heavenly Monarch") to guide his pen as he begins the first book of his epic poem about the life of Abraham.</meta>

# THE FIRST BOOK.
<meta>catchword: Give</meta>

Depart, depart to the side, before you might hinder my beginning;
I do not sing for you: I sing only for Abraham's children. <note>The author distinguishes his audience; he is not writing for skeptics or the "unbelieving," but for the faithful who consider themselves spiritual descendants of Abraham.</note>
I scatter my roses for the spiritual Seed
Of the great Father, who, with a reverent face,
Bows his humble soul, like full ears of grain,
Before the truth of the divine pages,
While slander rages, and blusters, glowing with wrath,
And grows like sterile grain on steeper stalks.
<margin>He calls upon the Godhead for assistance.</margin>
O Heavenly Monarch! whose eyes never sleep,
Who through your omnipotence created the great Universe!
Infinite Being! who, in the highest vault,
Has the most perfect object of your love in yourself,
And yet casts your eye upon man, and his works!
O Father of mercy! ah, please strengthen my spirit,
As I set your holy promise and wondrous dealings with
Your heart-friend <term>Abraham</term><note>Abraham is traditionally referred to as the "Friend of God" (Khalil Allah in Arabic tradition, and similarly in Judeo-Christian texts), signifying a unique intimacy with the Divine.</note> to biblical tones!
Ah! let a spark of the divine fire of love,
That pained You with compassion for man's salvation,
Inflame my soul with a pure heavenly glow!
Ah! let a single ray enlighten my mind;
A ray of wisdom, shot from the <term>Sun of Salvation</term>;<note>original: "Heilzon." A metaphorical reference to God or Christ as the source of spiritual light and healing.</note>
A light of truth, sprung from your sanctuary;
A radiance of soul-peace, from your all-sufficiency:
So that your splendor may be spread in my poem!
Lead me, O Sun of Salvation! on dark letter-paths! <note>The "dark letter-paths" refer to the difficult or obscure passages of ancient scripture that the poet must navigate.</note>
O Spirit! guide my spirit on the track of the holy pages!

<summary>The author begins "The First Book" by dedicating his work to the faithful and invoking God for divine inspiration and clarity to interpret the scriptures correctly.</summary>
<keywords>invocation, Abraham, Godhead, Sun of Salvation, Holy Promise, spiritual seed, divine inspiration</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the opening of the first book. Previously, the author invoked the Divine for inspiration to tell the story of Abraham. # ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.
<meta>catchword: It seemed</meta>

My Lord remembers (thus began that <term>Rose-mouth</term><note>original: "Rozemont." A conventional poetic epithet in 17th-century Dutch literature, meaning "Rose-mouth," used here to emphasize Sarah’s beauty despite her age.</note>)
How you, before we stepped onto Egypt’s fertile ground,
To dwell here for a time as strangers;
Revealed your fear to me, and the anxieties of your soul
For this beauty, which Heaven still preserves,
A gift of nature, but of a worrisome nature:
How you commanded that I should call myself your **Sister**;
So that they might not kill you because of me, and that we might
Live together more peacefully among this strange and wanton people.
This lies heavy on my heart: it seems to me I see a cloud
Of adversities, which I surely have to expect.
<margin>Concerning Abram's command that she should call herself his Sister.</margin>
I labor under mountains of anxious thoughts,
Sleep flees my eyes; or when it creeps upon me,
And now and then steals into my tired limbs,
Then I startle and awake from grim and fearful dreams.
Not that I shrink from following your commands,
My **Abram**, no, oh no; the sorrows I endure
Arise from the fear that you might die because of me.
Could this outward beauty still bring forth such great misfortune,
In my old age of sixty-five years?
May the <term>Most High</term><note>original: "d'Opperste." A reference to God.</note>, who appeared to you before, prevent that!
But this wondrous dream alone burdens my heart:
I dreamed that I, in the midst of the valleys,
Saw a beautiful rose parade in its full splendor:
It stood on display throughout the summer heat,
And remained, in scent and color and luster, just as beautiful.

<summary>Sarah expresses her deep anxiety to Abraham regarding their decision to tell the Egyptians she is his sister, fearing for his life and recounting an ominous yet beautiful dream of a resilient rose.</summary>
<keywords>Sarah, Abraham, Egypt, sister-wife motif, dream, beauty, anxiety, Rose-mouth, sixty-five years old</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues Sarah’s speech to Abraham as they enter Egypt. On the previous page, Sarah began describing a symbolic dream in which she saw herself as a beautiful rose in a valley. # THE FIRST BOOK.
<meta>catchword: My</meta>

It seemed as if Heaven, through a wondrous power,
Preserved that flower, so pleasant in everyone’s eyes,
From the autumn storms. It seemed to me I saw
How that rose lay quite tenderly and close to your heart;
But, when you thought to care for this flower the most,
You found it, alas! on an early morning,
Stolen from the garden, and snatched from its place.
I awoke, and this dream remains pressed upon my soul.
Here Lady **Sara** fell silent, and a deep sigh, flown
From her bosom, forced a stream of tears from her eyes,
Which flowed from her cheeks onto her white breast:
Just as the morning dew sprays the leaves wet,
While a breeze comes creeping into the flower garden,
And makes those pearls drip onto the rosebuds.
<margin>
*She is comforted, and put at ease.*
</margin>
The <term>pious Father</term><note>original: "vrome Vader." A traditional honorific for the patriarch Abraham.</note>, himself moved by this state,
Dries her tears, and presses her tender hand;
He comforts his spouse thus: take courage, my **Chosen One**,
I hope your dream will be but a dream—that is, vanity.
But if misfortune wills that the <term>wanton people</term><note>original: "dartle lantaart." This is likely an OCR error for "landaart" (people of the land/nation), referring to the Egyptians whom Abraham fears will be overcome by lust.</note> lurk
For your beauty, driven by a hot passion;
He <note>Referring to the ruler of the land.</note> will attempt to buy you through service,
Where otherwise violence might dissolve the <term>marriage bond</term><note>original: "huwlyksbant." Abraham fears that if he is known as her husband, the Egyptians will kill him to "legally" take her; by posing as her brother, he hopes to negotiate and delay.</note>.
My tender care for you has devised this invention:
If you are desired for marriage, you remain yet in my power.
One shall constantly stretch the wedding day longer,
Until we finally depart again from this place.

<summary>Sarah concludes her ominous dream of a stolen rose, weeping at the premonition; Abraham comforts her by explaining that their "sibling" ruse will allow him to delay any marriage proposals until they can safely leave Egypt.</summary>
<keywords>Sarah's dream, Abraham's plan, Egypt, marriage bond, rose metaphor, Pharaoh, patriarchal narrative</keywords>

### ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.
<meta>catchword: In</meta>

"My **Beautiful One**, be at ease; the all-seeing Godhead lives,
Who has guided us thus far, and has bountifully blessed us.
Let us cast our cares upon God’s omnipotence:
He shall send His light and clarity into the darkness,
And show, after this sorrow, His <term>saving countenance</term><note>original: "heilryk aangezicht." A poetic way of describing God’s favor and divine presence.</note>."
Thus he speaks. Through these words, **Sarah’s** heart is lightened.

<margin>Rumor spreads through Memphis of the arrival of a strange multitude of people.</margin>
The sun had three times, with a joyful and smiling countenance,
Clad in shining gold, risen in the east,
And had now three times, weary from her day's journey,
Extinguished her torch in the <term>sea of Libya</term><note>original: "zee van Libië." In the geography of this period, the Mediterranean or the waters to the west of Egypt were often referred to as the Libyan sea; here it signifies the sunset.</note>;
When swift **Rumor**, which, fertile in the spreading
Of new tidings, flew along the streets and meadows of **Memphis**,
Called out from a hundred mouths how, near the city,
A strange multitude had struck camp.
The country folk were questioned as they came to market with their wares.
One said: "It is native people; they are Egyptians
From **Coptos**<note>original: "Koptos." An ancient city on the Nile that served as a major trading hub for goods coming from the Red Sea.</note>, accustomed to coming here with their grain."
Another: "They are **Arabs** with camels, heavily laden
With cloves, cinnamon, and the scent of incense for offerings,
And balsam perfumes for the hair of courtly ladies."
Yet another mingles something else into the mix.
Thus lies run, through the ignorant and wild commoners,
Constantly ahead of the truth, with unequal strides:
Just as an unbridled horse prances with swift limbs,
And runs and neighs, until it stops before a ditch,
And, exhausted, awaits its embarrassed master.

# ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.

And evening already cast its pale veil;
Before the Princes, once more welcome to the King,
Appear in the court, where **Pharaoh**, to dine,
Was seated in a spacious hall at the evening table.
The courtly youths form a wide circle
Around the table, and the <term>grand chamberlain</term><note>original: "weitsche kamerling." A high-ranking official in charge of the royal household.</note>

A row of lords, who pour the fresh **nectar**<note>A poetic reference to fine wine, comparing the Pharaoh's drink to the drink of the gods.</note>,

The tasters, refined of tongue, and fastidious of delicacies.
Sovereign **Pharaoh** commands the Great Lords to step forward,
And to dechypher for him the truth of the confused rumor.

Spreads through far-famed **Egypt** and the East,
No enemy, intent on plunder, came thundering into your realm,

**Egypt** lives, by your governance, in rest and peace:
But foreign shepherds, with household and cattle,

Into the land of **Goshen**<note>original: "Goze." The region in Egypt assigned to the Israelites.</note>, to live there for a short time.

Lest any cruel wolf should surprise the sheep.

The shepherd boy sings a song, or plays upon his flute,

## THE FIRST BOOK.
B 2
<meta>catchword: In</meta>

Or upon the <term>shepherd’s reed</term><note>original: "herdersriet." A simple flute or pipe, often used in pastoral poetry to signify a state of peace and harmony with nature.</note>, at which even the winds fall silent,
The birds listen and the fields gain ears.
It is as if sweet peace itself dwelt there in the open field.
<margin>And they extol the beauty of Sarah.</margin>
But that which has most amazed and unsettled your servants,
Is the lovely countenance of an elderly Shepherdess,
A woman truly worthy of the King’s love.
Her dignified manner shows a graceful majesty,
While a lovely rose-red spreads across her cheeks.
Her face is modest yet full of lively charms,
Like the bright sapphires in a Sovereign’s crown:
It is as if nature had reserved her for something magnificent,
And saved such beauty for your royal eyes.
She is tall of stature, and graceful in her limbs:
Whenever she steps from her tent into the open field,
Everyone would say she resembles a Goddess,
Adorned in her finest attire upon her feast day.
Her name is Sarah, and the old Shepherd
She respectfully calls Lord, or **Abram**, or her brother. <note>This refers to the biblical account in Genesis 12, where Abraham asks Sarah to identify as his sister to ensure his safety in Egypt.</note>

<margin>Their flattery ignites love in the breast of the King.</margin>
The praise of this Beauty awakens love in his aging breast;
It ignites the heart and flows through the Sovereign’s veins;
Indeed, it rises higher, until the passions, having taken flight,
Spread across his face and sparkle from his eyes;
Like a fire that strikes within a high temple,
Steadily growing, glowing wherever it touches and spreading further,
And gnaws at the ceiling, and makes the pillars creak,
Until the bright flame flares up through the roof.

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative following the princes' report to Pharaoh. Having been captivated by the description of Sarah’s beauty on the previous page, Pharaoh now takes action to bring her to his court, prompting a moralizing reflection by the author on the dangers of flattery.</meta>

->### ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.<-
<meta>catchword: While</meta>

<margin>He desires to have Sarah at the court.</margin>
At last Pharaoh says: "I desire that they go once more
To Goshen, and fetch the beautiful Sarah here:
I feel a wondrous desire to behold that fresh countenance:
Lead her into the palace among the court's <term>ladies-in-waiting</term><note>original: "staatjonkvrouwen." Noblewomen who serve at a royal court.</note>."
He takes a golden cup full of <term>Meroitic wine</term><note>original: "Meroëëfchen wyn." Wine from Meroë, an ancient city on the east bank of the Nile in modern-day Sudan, famous in antiquity for its wealth and luxury.</note>,
And says: "This shall be to the health of that foreign beauty."
Now a cheer is heard from a thousand loud throats,
While the court music begins to delight the ear.

<margin>Flattery is the corruption of princes.</margin>
O wicked Flattery, hated enemy
Of true sincerity, who through a feigned love
Betrays majesty, and intends to attack the walls
Of the princely mind where it is weakest:
You honor the rising sun when it shines brightly;
But when a cloud obscures her, then you disappear
Like a shadow; yes, you shoot your evil arrows
When she sets, and can no longer light your way;
What mischief have you brought forth in the world!
How often have you violated heroic virtue,
When you know how to advise Princes toward wickedness?
What has innocent virtue not suffered through your evil deeds,
O wicked Deceiver? Had you not seduced **Adam**,
Perhaps humanity would still stand in its primal innocence.

<margin>They set out by night for the abduction;</margin>
The ignited passion rages ever more within.
The beautiful Sarah floats through the King's heart and senses:
And that unholy fire is steadily in his mind
Cherished and fed by courtly flattery.

<summary>Pharaoh orders Sarah to be brought to his palace from Goshen, celebrating her beauty with a toast. The author interjects with a stern warning against the "wickedness of flattery," which corrupts the hearts of rulers and leads them away from virtue.</summary>

# THE FIRST BOOK.
B 3
<meta>catchword: For</meta>

While the Nobles, chosen for the abduction,
Now with a much greater retinue approach the land of **Goshen**<note>A fertile region in the Nile Delta where Abraham’s family was encamped.</note>,
While the morning light was still expected at the horizon.
Treachery always chooses the dark night,
Because her wicked deed cannot endure the daylight.
The morning light also began to dawn later;
The sun climbed more slowly onto the morning firmament, perhaps
Out of sheer dread to see such an abduction of a woman.

<margin>Which is accomplished in the early morning;</margin>
God-fearing **Abram**, having now risen before the dew and before daybreak,
To offer his prayers to the infinite <term>Supreme Being</term><note>original: "Opperwezen." A term for God emphasizing His status as the highest authority.</note>,
Before the breaking of the light, in the nearby woods,
Where silence and solitude best keep him in contemplation;
Was now in the midst of his holy reflections,
When the courtly household came crowding into the humble field-hut,
And abducted **Sara**, far from any respect or reverence.
She cried: "O Heaven, help! Ah! **Abram**, **Abram**, ah!
See how I am stolen!" But **Abram** could not hear.
All the field echoed, and woods and mountains gained ears.
The irrational cattle jumped suddenly from their sleep.
The pious **Lot** with all his house, and shepherd boy,
And servant, and maid, they all came running out of the tent in amazement,
While the robbers spurred the steeds through the field,
And chaste **Sara**, who strikes her white breast in grief,
Is carried to **Memphis**<note>The ancient capital of Egypt and the seat of Pharaoh's power.</note> in the chariot of the Prince.
A thick cloud of dust covered her from view.
Go on, Oppressor, and remain quite unmoved—

<meta>This page continues the narrative following the abduction of Sarah.
