-> ## ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH. <-

By turns his spirit also becomes weak and faint in strength.
Like a traveler, who in the dreary night
Pursues his way, and, overtaken by a thunderstorm,
Is seized by distress within his anxious heart:
Now he sees the right track, through the bright flickering light
Of the lightning bolts, which to his startled sight
Show his dwelling place, and his fatherly roofs;
But soon another clap of thunder begins to crack:
Then he rubs his eyes in a double darkness,
And doubts whether he is indeed on the right path.

<margin>He is comforted by Lot.</margin>
In this condition the pious **Lot** came to find him,
And cried: "O **Abram**, stand now firm in the storm-winds
Of adversity, like a rock in a barren sea.
Keep courage, my <term>fellow-sufferer</term><note>original: "Lotgenoot." This is likely a poetic play on words by the author, as "Lot" is both the name of the speaker and the Dutch word for "fate" or "destiny."</note>, I see your salvation already,
Like the dawn, springing forth from the east.
The Almighty shall shortly comfort us with joy.
He exercises our virtue, so that it may be patient.
Let one walk quietly through the parched wilderness
Of His trial, and let one learn to endure the <term>strokes of love</term><note>original: "liefdeslagen." A common theological concept of the era suggesting that God's discipline or the trials He allows are actually signs of His love and care for the soul's refinement.</note>
From our Father, with a steadfast patience."

<margin>And trusts in God's promises.</margin>
The troubled passion is now finally more calmed.
The <term>sun of reason</term><note>original: "redezon." A metaphor typical of Enlightenment-era religious writing, suggesting that divine logic and clarity are beginning to pierce through the "clouds" of human emotion.</note> breaks through. The understanding is cleared.
That language falls lovely into the heart of **Abram**;
Like a gentle dew, in the hot summer weather,
Upon the half-scorched grass of evening descends.
"No," (he says) "**son of Haran**<note>Lot was the son of Haran, who was Abram's brother.</note>, I have not yet gone astray;"

I sail by the <term>lodestar</term><note>original: "leiftar." A guiding star used for navigation, here a metaphor for divine guidance.</note> of God’s holy promises:

Everyone has something new to say; this one listens, that one murmurs;
Another stands as if overwhelmed by amazement;
While a tenfold rumor, from mouth to mouth,
Floats through the wild commoners, and wanders around,
Which every moment, through so many other reports,
Mingles itself, according to the nature of the fickle **Egyptians**.

And three times fear keeps his lips sealed tight.

# THE FIRST BOOK.

Will then the curse, which lies upon this race of Ham <note>original: "Chams geslachte". In the biblical tradition of the time, the Egyptians were viewed as descendants of Ham, Noah’s son, who was cursed.</note>,

While a whirlwind strikes us here and there,
And moves our mind to perverse steps,
According to the passion of human qualities!
<margin>He calls upon the Godhead for help.</margin>

And not long after, from His azure halls,
He let a soft clap of thunder descend from afar.
This restores courage and strength to the heart of my great Man.
Now his holy faith stands once more in full radiance.

# P R E F A C E.

...[orig]inated <note>The Dutch text begins mid-word with "sprongen," likely completing "ontsprongen" (originated or sprouted).</note> glimmer in the investigation of deep antiquities and circumstances which the sacred Writers pass over in silence, and the secular <note>original: "onheiligen" (unholy), here referring to non-biblical or profane historians.</note> writers mention only with an abundance of trifles and a lack of consistency: for which reason we, in treating this subject matter, alongside the Theologians <term>Godtgeleerden</term> <note>Scholars of divinity or theology</note>, have not seldom had to consult probability and guess cautiously: two liberties which no one (since the interpreters of the sacred pages claim them for themselves) can rightly refuse the Poet; for he is free, according to the nature and character of his art, like the Draftsman <term>Teekenaar</term> and Painter <term>Schilder</term>, to adorn his scene according to the invention of his spirit with frames, foliage, and accessory work; to paint space into his piece through pleasant vistas and distances; and even, when in sacred subject matter he keeps the Truth in the light and in the foreground, to cast light and shadow as the arrangement of his artistic work requires.

This would be enough said to the poetic Reader concerning the liberty of Poetry; but since we suspect that the mere name of **Abraham** will draw many Pious but Unlearned <note>Those with religious devotion but lacking formal academic or literary training.</note> folk to leaf through the life of this illustrious Patriarch <term>Geloofsvader</term> <note>Literally "Father of Faith," a traditional title for Abraham in Dutch Reformed and Christian tradition.</note>, I find it not inadvisable also [to provide] some

<summary>In this preface, the author justifies using poetic license to fill in historical gaps in the life of Abraham, arguing that a poet, much like a painter, may use imagination and "light and shadow" to enhance a sacred narrative without obscuring its truth.</summary>
<keywords>Abraham, Poetic License, Art Theory, Theology, Biblical Narrative, Preface, Patriarch</keywords>

# P R E F A C E.

*to clear away the stumbling blocks from before their feet: or to lead them past them by the hand.*

It is clear that our History has not been recorded everywhere with equal detail by the Divine *Moses*, and while more is kept silent in one place, more is reported in another. The abduction <term>schaking</term> <note>Referring to the biblical account in Genesis 12 where Sarah is taken into Pharaoh's palace.</note> of *Sarah* to the Egyptian court—with which we begin our piece and which therefore required more extensive circumstances—provided us with these only sparingly; yet there are clear signs of where these events, though unmentioned, occurred more broadly. When King *Pharaoh*, after the abduction, gives *Abraham* gifts and treats him well for the sake of his sister *Sarah*, one clearly sees that an interval and postponement of the marriage occurred, and the reason why that forced union <term>echt</term> was not consummated remains unmentioned. Many researchers of Biblical antiquities have also observed this and guessed at the cause of this delay; one can find various conjectures about it in the works of the highly learned *W. Goeree* <note>Willem Goeree (1635–1711), a prominent Dutch bookseller and author known for his "Mosaize Historie" (1683), which mixed biblical history with secular archaeology.</note>, in his *Mosaic Histories*; yet all are without any certainty or proof. For this reason, I believe I may, with equal right, propose that the festival <term>hoogtyt</term> <note>A "high time" or major religious festival.</note> of *Isis*, celebrated so solemnly by the Egyptians, intervened—a time which forbade all joy until the Ox-god *Apis* <note>The sacred bull of Memphis, worshipped as a god; his "appearance" or discovery was a cause for great celebration.</note> revealed himself: nevertheless

<summary>In this preface, the author explains that while the biblical account of Sarah's abduction by Pharaoh is brief, the gaps in the narrative allow for poetic and historical speculation. He suggests that a religious festival for Isis and Apis might have been the reason the marriage was delayed, preventing the union before Sarah was returned.</summary>

Not one in thousands can lift their eyes upward,

### THE SECOND BOOK.

And I launch my poetic vessel <note>original: "dichthulk"; a "hulk" was a type of heavy merchant ship. The poet views his writing process as a metaphorical voyage.</note>, (for Heaven hears my prayer)

<margin>Idolatry described;</margin>
The descendants of Ham, having strayed from the holy faith
That Father Noah used to preach to his sons,
And from the religion which he, as the most precious cargo
In the first-built ship <note>The Ark.</note>, brought out of the first world;
Had long since lost the conception of the true God,
And chosen false gods according to their own foolish whims.
They had all sorts of gods, monstrous in their form,
Both gods of the house and hearth, and protectors of the land,
And portable gods, whom the innocent, wherever they went,
Religiously honored, hanging them from their necks and belts,
Of every shape and make, carved
With animal heads upon human limbs,
Which the cunning priesthood gave to the foolish Egyptians,
Under the pretense of curing sickness and warding off dangers,
Placing them in their hands, and with secret symbolism <note>original: "beeldespraak"; refers to the allegorical or hieroglyphic nature of Egyptian religion as understood in the 18th century.</note>
Strengthened them against all the violence of Typhon’s <note>original: "Thifons"; Typhon is the Greek name for the Egyptian god Set, often associated with chaos and storms.</note> revenge.

<margin>Specifically the service to the goddess Isis,</margin>
But above all those monstrous idol-puppets,
With heads of dogs, birds, and dreaded lions,
Forged in the priestly brain, the Egyptian also served,
With offerings and with solemn churchly gestures,
Their great temple-gods, to whom they offered incense.
But Isis, Queen and mother of the gods,
Possessed the fire and hearth, and was most highly praised by everyone,
Called the Heavenly Princess, and Lady of the Earth;

# ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.
<meta>catchword: Osiris</meta>

The Progenitor of Nature, the Queen of spirits,
Common Womb of men and beasts,
Arbiter of the seasons, who gives blessing to the earth,
And first discovered the use of wine and grain;
Yes, with an even greater series of titles and names
They greeted Isis, when they came into her temple,
And offered incense, and poured out their prayers;
For Isis gave everything: yes, Isis alone was
The salvation, the fertility, for youthful spouses;
The hope of women, from whom they received the power of childbirth.
It is said that she of old, paired with the god Osiris <note>original: "Osyr"; a shortened form used here for poetic meter.</note>,
Had given birth to the gods Harpocrates and Horus:
That Typhon <note>original: "Thifon"; the Greek name for the Egyptian god Set, often associated with chaos and storms.</note>, the Idol of evil and of sins;
<margin>And the origin of Apis,</margin> Murdered her husband Osiris with many wounds,
And that she, in deep mourning, sought her spouse's limbs <note>This refers to the central Egyptian myth where Set dismembers Osiris and scatters his body parts across the land.</note>,
Which, finding them at last, she brought to the grave full of sorrow:
But that (which sounds like a miracle) an ox then appeared,
In which the divinity of Osiris visibly dwelt;
Which presently, displayed with great joy for all to see,
Was worshipped, and protected from the bold violence
Of the cruel Typhon, who, when the vengeance
Of the angry Isis crushed him, moved into a Crocodile <note>original: "Krokedil"; In various versions of the myth, Set takes the form of dangerous animals like crocodiles or hippopotamuses.</note>.
It is also said that this beast was then named Apis,
And his oracle was famous throughout Egypt.
But this is certain: that among the Egyptians,
On Isis's feast day, with solemn religious gestures,

# THE SECOND BOOK.

<sig>F</sig>
<meta>catchword: One</meta>

...mourned the death of Osiris, and how, after the lamentation,
The joyful appearance of the Bull-god <note>original: "Osgodt"; the Apis bull, which the Egyptians believed was the living incarnation of the god Osiris.</note> was celebrated.
<margin>And their feast day, which is now being celebrated;</margin>
This was the high festival that Memphis was now solemnly observing.
The time of mourning did not permit one to adorn oneself for joy.
The final day, when that god was to appear,
Was intended for the joy of the King’s second marriage <note>This refers to the Pharaoh's intention to marry Sarah, whom he believes to be Abraham's sister.</note>.
Now one hears, day after day, in solemn processions,
The cruel murder of Osiris in songs of mourning and grief
Lamented: everyone shows their distress and inner pain:
The whole court is in mourning, the temples are hung
With black, and one sees the King, and his kin,
The nobles, and the Council, now wearing myrtle branches.
Thus state pomp and luster are set aside.
The courtly retinue follows the procession with a slow step.
Heads hang low, and weapons trail along.
All Memphis follows, their hearts constricted with distress:
But Isis’s priesthood, dressed in white garments,
Who beat their anxious breasts with a dismal sound,
Have, as a sign of distress, disfigured their faces;
Their heads are shaved, and their bodies with deep wounds
Are cut; they wail and sigh, and moan from fearful need,
In honor of Isis’s grief, and of Osiris’s death.
They hold pine branches or wormwood sprigs in their hands.
The Sistrum <term>Sistrum: (original: "Sister") A rattling percussion instrument used in ancient Egyptian religious ceremonies, particularly those for Isis, to ward off evil spirits.</term> rattles, and strikes its copper edges,
Now driving away Typhon’s spirit <note>original: "Thiſons geeſt"; Typhon is the Greek name for Set, the Egyptian god of chaos who murdered Osiris.</note> with the harsh and dismal sound,
And coaxes the sighs of the people from their breasts.

# ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.
<meta>catchword: Thus</meta>

Because Osiris, who manifested himself in Apis,
Ruled that light, and crowned the land with fertility.
When the sun had now just risen to the horizons,
Every heart was constricted, as if between hope and fear,
Where a thousand eyes stare from the high spheres;
But she had hardly, from a sea of golden waves,
Lifted her perfect face above the water,
Which splashes golden drops into the radiant being,
Than the Bull-god <note>original: "Osgodt"; the Apis bull, which the Egyptians believed was the living incarnation of the god Osiris.</note> approached, and began to low;
The copper temple door creaked and groaned,
And slowly opened. Now Apis arrives,
With silver horns, neatly curved like the moon;
He steps out of the temple, accompanied by all the ranks
Of Isis’s priests, who lead him through the streets.

<margin>General joy of the multitude.</margin>
Now all the people raise their voices at once in joy.
The city is full of cheering. Happiness reaches its peak.
They wave their veils—and whatever else had represented their distress <note>The veils used during the previous days of mourning are now waved in celebration.</note>—
Now over their heads. It now turns to opulence.
Everyone sings, and jumps, and cries: blessed is the day
That one may find Osiris again in Apis!
It echoes with Isis’s praise, and Apis’s great power.
Thus the multitude follows, to where, before their eyes,
The fragrant sacrificial wine, mixed with earth and salt,
Is poured upon his white spot <note>original: "kol"; a distinctive white marking on the forehead of the otherwise black Apis bull.</note>, on the black forehead.
There a costly garment, from top to bottom
Embroidered with precious gold, is hung around his limbs.

Thus he is led to the sanctuary, where he has his dwelling,

<margin>The folly of the people and the deceit of the Priests are the pillars of a false religion.</margin>
How the ignorant people wander in a dark misery,
When they lack the rays of divine Truth,
And depend too piteously on the lips

Whereby the innocent people, too piteously misled,
Let themselves be blinded by the hypocrisy of those cunning ones,
Who find only self-interest in all the ceremonies!

<margin>The sorrow of Sarah in these circumstances.</margin>
But beautiful Sarah, who in the large and princely house
Now hears the shouting of the people and the jubilant festival noise,
And every moment feels constricted by terror
For the hour when she will be dragged before the marriage altar;
Is now at her wit's end; and her bewildered heart
Almost faints through despair in that pious grief.
Now fear and anxiety come sweeping into her breast:
Then everything must give way to her marital love;
And sometimes she is again overcome by her sorrow,
While she often forges a courageous resolution.
But, wherever she turns in her uncertain thoughts,
She melts in tears, or bursts out in mournful complaints;
Like a thick mass of clouds, compressed
By whirlwinds, is squeezed until it bursts,

At the cost of the head of my Abram, oh Sorrow!
Who knows not the passion of a King in love?
I feel a cold terror driven through all my veins.
Ah me, wretched woman, who helps me from these dangers;
Since Heaven, earth, and even terrible death,

<margin>She falls into a swoon, after which she takes a noble resolution:</margin>
Here she sank into a faint, with the color of death <note>original: "dootverf"; a term for a deathly pallor, or the initial pale layer of a painting.</note> upon her lips:
It seemed as if the soul would now senselessly slip away from her:
She pressed her breathless breast with her head:
Like a fresh flower, by no one's hand defiled,
Bows its head forward, and lies sunk to the earth,
By the sun's scorch, and by lack of dew and rain:
At last she recovers, and says with an anxious sigh,
Which her fearful soul breathes out into the air:
All this comes upon me, less through the raging of my abductor,
Than through the poison of this supposed beauty.
It is best that I take revenge on that vain outward beauty,
And break that varnish <note>original: "vernis"; used here metaphorically to describe her physical attractiveness as a mere surface coating.</note> with my frantic hands,
Defile it, and let the hair hang unloosed around my head;
So that the King might be disgusted by my torn cheeks.
I shall the headdress <term>hulfel: a traditional ornamental cap or head-covering</term>, and the princely bridal attire,

I shall risk everything for religion and my morals;
For noble freedom, and my Abram’s marriage covenant <note>original: "trouverbont"; referring to the sacred bond of marriage.</note>.

The King (she said) wishes a blessed morning

But before the golden sun shall rise three times more,
And three times descend into the moist evening horizons,
Pharaoh hopes to set the crown upon your head;
If what a line of physicians promises him be true.

She recognizes in the King’s illness a divine providence; <note>original: "beleit"; meaning the plan, management, or guiding hand of God.</note>

Awakens tears of joy with pleasant gusts.

# THE SECOND BOOK.

G

<meta>catchword: The Egyp-</meta>

Just as after northern storms in the lovely season of May,
By which the trees tremble from the cold, and the youthful green
Withers <note>original: "besterft"; literally "dies away," used here to describe the fading of color and life in plants due to frost or wind.</note> from the barren wind; until from the balmy south
A gentle rain-shower upon the first-budding herbs
And flowers drips, and sprinkles all the crops in the field,
And thus restores the faint hope of the husbandman.
<margin>The entire court is afflicted by the Plague.</margin>
But what a dark cloud of grim and black cares
Hangs over the head of Memphis! <note>The capital of ancient Egypt during the Old Kingdom.</note> Every morning one hears
Distressing tidings, which sow fear and anxiety and terror
Among the bustling people, and grow with every moment,
As they are carried further upon the tongues of men.
Nothing else is spoken of but the cruel plagues,
And of the hidden evil that creeps upon Pharaoh,
And into all the Nobility, day by day, steals into their veins;
It makes the blood ferment, the heart in restless nights
Oppressed by torment and melancholy thoughts;
It tortures the limbs and douses the spark of life.
The Nile Prince <note>original: "Nylvorst"; a poetic title for the Pharaoh, emphasizing his rule over the life-giving river.</note> lay now exhausted upon his couch;
Where his household and court, his kin and his friends,
Find themselves in the same misery and grim condition.
Luxury was now gone, and excess had departed;
Splendor and pride were almost crushed into the dust.
Fair-haired Pleasure, <note>original: "blonde wellust"; pleasure or luxury personified as an attractive, fair-haired figure.</note> who used to stroke the King
With soft hands, was—along with all her playmates,
The delicacies, and the sluggish idleness,
Delight and amusement—banished from the court by the plague.

The plagues, which now grow steadily day by day,

Lady Hoffra, who alone of Pharaoh’s lineage
Remained free from the plague, came in the dark of night,
(A mother’s love <note>original: "moedermin"; refers to maternal affection or a mother's devotion.</note> fears neither night nor darkness)
To her Son, who now because of pain must go without sleep.
She says, with tears in her eyes: "Great Prince,
Born from this womb, suckled at this breast,
If you still trust anything to my gray hairs,
And the experience of a multitude of years,
Hear then my faithful counsel in this anxious time:
It would not be the first time that my love and diligence
Snatched you from death's danger and evilly forged snares."
The King keeps his eyes fixed upon her face,
And feels a wondrous stirring in his soul,
Which makes the heart burn with a tender glow of love.
He spoke: "Most worthy Lady, do you still know how to give counsel,
Where even Wisdom is silent? I swear to you by my life,
Which I received from you, and by the high gods
Whom we worship, by my scepter and my crown;
By all that is holy; that I shall follow your counsel!"
<margin>And rebukes her Son for the Abduction;</margin> <note>original: "Schaking"; the act of taking a woman by force or deception, in this case, Sarah.</note>

And at your glorious house: I have discovered the evil,
The crime that has brought the plague upon your head." <note>original: "op uwen hals verwekt"; literally "brought upon your neck," an idiom for bringing punishment upon oneself.</note>

### THE SECOND BOOK.

G 3
<meta>catchword: In</meta>

"The Sarah, whom you have chosen as your Bride,
Is Abram's lawful wife, destined for him by the gods:
You have seized her by force, brought her to the court,
And violated the law of nations <note>original: "recht der volkeren"; referring to the *jus gentium*, the concept of universal laws shared by all civilized peoples, specifically hospitality.</note> and hospitality.
A King, just as much as his subjects, must attend
To justice and reason, from which he never outgrows:
Or if he ever dares, too foolish and enraged,
To violate the sacred right that he must protect,
Then the gods enter into judgment, who through plagues
Avenge the innocent, and summon the Prince to the tribunal: <note>original: "vierfchaar"; a historical Dutch term for a court of law or a place of judgment.</note>
Therefore I advise you, my Son, if you love your well-being,
And fear our gods, that you conquer your passion,
And return this Sarah to her Abram;
Thus shall your plagues and miseries soon end."

<margin>
*Whereupon*
*he promises*
*Sarah's*
*release.*
</margin>
The Prince of the Nile <note>original: "Nylvorst"; a poetic title for the Pharaoh.</note> is astonished, and feels his spirit tangled
In firm snares that grip him around the heart.
Love, sorrow, pain, anxiety, fear, and rage,
And the holy oath he had just sworn,
Each contended most fiercely within his conquered soul,
Until, gasping, he fell into a deathly faint.
Like a hart <note>A male deer.</note> which, struck by an arrow while running,
Out of deathly terror, knowing neither thicket nor leafy woods,
Tries to flee its deep wounds, until the net
Of the cunning hunter ensnares the wretched animal;
And the more strongly it struggles to save its life,
The sooner, faint of strength, it must surrender itself as conquered.

At last the Prince sighs, and cries out, full of deep sorrow:
"Alas! Is Sarah then the shepherd Abram’s Wife?
And do I suffer this because of her? Then my love must yield.
But you, O Heaven! who plagues me, you can bear witness,

But with my own hand I shall give her back to Abram.

And let my healing set the seal upon this decree!"

### ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.

And all his limbs with a heart-opening sap;
Full of new spirits <note>original: "geesten"; in the 18th century, "animal spirits" were believed to be fluid substances that carried life and sensation through the body</note>, of a wondrous quality,
And divine power; that flowed like a balm,
And sprinkled the weary body like a morning dew.
Thus gentle Sleep pressed in, in the middle of the night,
Decorated with poppy leaves <note>the poppy was a classical symbol associated with Morpheus, the god of sleep</note>, prompted by a higher power,
Through the whole court, as far as the evil plague had pressed,
And had assailed the noble lineage of the King <note>Referring to Pharaoh, whose household was struck with "great plagues" because of Sarah, as described in Genesis 12</note>.

<margin>By which the body is strengthened, and the plague disappears</margin>
The next day, one feels the sluggish heart strengthened.
Evil must give way when the Deity works alongside.
The Prince awakes, freed from pain and grim worries,
Refreshed like a rose, which, in the cool morning,
Lifts its half-withered head up from the dust again.
Pale fear vanishes; hope appears at the court.
Sweet joy, which had to yield before the danger,
Appeared, and showed itself on a thousand faces,
And flattered Memphis <note>one of the ancient capitals of Egypt</note> that it would soon be delivered
From the disaster that had already cost princely blood.
Just as the dense forest, which through the night by thunderstorms
And wind-hurricanes, burst forth from the south,
Was shaken to and fro, lashed all around;
At the joyful dawning of a quiet morning hour,
Is refreshed, and filled again by the spirited warbling
Of a thousand little birds, that soar through the branches;
While here and there one sees an oak torn down,
The branches ripped away, and the green foliage crushed.

# ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.

<meta>catchword: Here</meta>

The modest gaze and the features of his face

His age, of more than ten times seven years <note>The patriarch is described here as being over seventy years old, consistent with the biblical account of his travels</note>,
Had indeed furrowed his countenance, and turned his hair
To silver, but, by divine command, time's hand had never

<margin>Pharaoh’s address to Abram,</margin>
Thus my Abram now steps magnanimously into the light:
He arrives where Pharaoh lay upon the purple daybed <note>original: "purpren rustbed"; purple was an expensive dye associated with royalty and high status in the ancient world</note>,

You, Stranger, whose arrival has, for these past few weeks,
Cost me so dearly in misfortune, pain, and torment;
What is this? Answer for yourself: what have you done to me;
Why did you not send messengers to me immediately,
<margin>With sharp reproach.</margin>
To let me know that you, joined in marriage,
Lived with Sarah, and that she was your lawful Wife?
Why did you say, forsaking your truth,
That she was your Sister; so that I, without hesitation,
Had her taken, and had accepted her as my Wife?
Had not Heaven prevented such adultery;
Is the honor of a Wife thus offered for sale in your land
For the favor of Princes? Or can one trade in such a way there,
And thus, through lying, remain in the favor of the gods?
Or were you brought up without gods or religion
Among wild peoples, where the truth is violated?

### THE THIRD BOOK.

H 2

<margin>Abram’s answer.</margin>
Here the King fell silent, and the pious Man, moved,
Bowed his head three times, and spoke, with downcast eyes:
O Great Prince of the Nile, hear the truth from the mouth
Of your humble Servant, who never violated the truth;
Who shall deceive no one, and especially no King.
We shepherds are not raised so savagely that we should lie,
Or live without Religion; no, my God, who lives forever,
And created all the world through His power,
Is Truth itself, and shall be my witness.
My Wife Sarah was my Sister long before this.
My Sister Sarah has become my Wife:
She was, O Prince, begotten in my Father’s second marriage <note>original: "geteelt in 's Vaders tweede trou"; Abram is explaining that Sarah is his half-sister, a detail used in the Biblical narrative (Genesis 20:12) to justify his claim that he spoke a partial truth rather than a total falsehood</note>:
I have not lied: but concealed a part of the truth,
Out of fear for my life: no law ever spoke against this.
I have also never offered her honor for sale for profit.
But I wished thus to preserve us both from the violence
Of an unchaste love, which her beautiful face might kindle,
In common laborers or low-ranking Egyptians:
But I never thought that Prince Pharaoh would
Set his mind upon a simple Shepherdess <note>original: "slechte Herderin"; here "slechte" means humble or of low social standing, rather than "bad" or "poor quality"</note>:
Therefore, at my wit's end and fearful for my precious life,
I gave up my case in silence to God's governance;
For God, whom I love, is pure Majesty,
Who leads the hearts of Kings like streams of water <note>A reference to the biblical proverb (Proverbs 21:1) suggesting that even the most powerful earthly rulers are subject to divine will</note>,
And inclines them wherever He pleases; yes, at His pleasure
All the mountains tremble, and the land is filled with plagues;

## ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.

Snatched from the idolatrous fire, carried out of the blaze; <note>original: "’t Afgodisch vuur ontrukt"; a reference to the Jewish tradition (Midrash) that Nimrod cast Abram into a furnace for his monotheism, from which God miraculously saved him.</note>

He raises his voice, and says, with eyes cast upward:
<margin>Blesses Pharaoh.</margin>
The God of Heaven, who, infinite in power,
Rules the great Universe, and from whose Majesty
The Kings of the earth all their might
Do derive, shall, O Prince! bless you for this deed,
And meet your descendants with His salvation and favor.
And if the wondrous light, which now shines upon my spirit,
Descends from His Omniscience and Wisdom;
<margin>And predicts for him deliverance from a famine through his descendant.</margin>

Shall bear no grain, and the husbandman, each year
Disappointed in his hope, shall bind no sheaves,
Nor find his reward for his bitter labor.
But Heaven’s providence, which no man can comprehend,
Shall inspire the brain of that wondrous Man,
Whom God shall raise up as a savior of Egypt;
Who again must serve as a pattern for a greater Savior <note>The author uses Joseph as a "type" or foreshadowing of Jesus Christ, a common theological theme in 18th-century literature.</note>:
He shall gather the abundance of ripe and golden-yellow grain,
Which for seven years long shall cover your fields,
With great supply into the country’s granary,
As food for the people in seven dry years.

<meta>This page continues the poetic description of Abraham’s pastoral life in Canaan. Having returned from Egypt, he shuns the luxury of palaces for a simple, virtuous life in nature, which is presented as a temple for the worship of God.</meta>

## ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

The starry heaven is his pavilion, beneath which
He sits, in contemplation of God’s greatness and the wondrous
Ingenuity of the Almighty: the field is the floor-carpet,
Embroidered with flowers, upon which he peacefully treads.
The vineyard pours him must <note>original: "most," unfermented or young wine</note>; the earth provides him food;
Simplicity sets the table in a friendly manner,
Where he lives joyfully with his Sarah,
And diligent activity gives them taste and appetite;
While the little birds, through a thousand organ-throats,
With more than royal music, caress their ears.

<margin>Humility praised above Ambition.</margin>
Give way, proud Ambition <note>original: "Staatzucht," meaning a lust for power or political status</note>, with your vain courtly followers,
The false greatness, delusion, deceit, and self-love,
Before noble Humility and her companions of the field,
Sweet Peace and Rest, which caress the heart and senses:
You may freely walk, unstable, upon sharp hills;
While she treads safely on soft rose petals,
And nourishes free virtue in the shepherd’s life:
You may give the Sovereigns their imagined greatness,
Blinding their eyes with the glittering gold of crowns,
While you forge chains and build dismal dungeons,
To lead the royal mind into slavery
Of evil deeds, which stir the heart with anxiety;
Torturing the conscience, while, in thick crowds,
Black cares and troublesome interests of state,
Press through the purple robes of state into their bosoms,
And surround them more faithfully than the bodyguard:

<summary>This page uses pastoral imagery to contrast Abraham’s humble, godly life in nature with the corrupt and anxiety-ridden lives of kings and the ambitious. It portrays nature as a royal palace superior to any man-made structure.</summary>

But Humility frees the spirit from slavery,
And subjects even my Abram to Abram’s own rule,
Which he governs like a prince, binding those monsters of the realm:
The wily passions and covetous desires;
So that his free soul may live in peace with God,
And soar toward heaven on the wings of faith.

<margin>Abraham's Wealth</margin>
Lean Poverty, which out of necessity and impotence
Humiliates man before our eyes against his will,
Also flees from Abram and his abundance:
The Almighty Sovereign, the Father of all good,
From whom gifts and perfect favors descend,
Has blessed him, from the azure halls of heaven,
With livestock and property, with white silver and fine gold,
With all the goods that mankind holds dear,
With menservants, maidservants, and with male and female slaves.
The joyful harvest month <note>original: "oogstmaant," the traditional Dutch name for August</note> lets him gain ripe fruits,
And fills the vat with wine, the barn with nourishing grain;

<margin>And Prosperity</margin>
Fertility dwells within his tents; the livestock increase
In great numbers; no cow has ever miscarried;
No ewe-lamb drops her lambs too early in the hedgerows;
No disease strikes the sheep or spoils the white wool;
The seed is never gnawed or rooted up
By a mouse or blind mole; no weevil <note>original: "klander," a type of beetle that infests stored grain</note> eats the corn;
No ant, anxious about scarcity, wanders through the furrows.
Thus prosperity sits here upon a golden throne,

<margin>In which Lot also shares.</margin>

# THE FOURTH BOOK.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

They rage together relentlessly, stirring up new wrath: some seek to pull their friends from mortal danger, while others attempt, with a well-meaning ruse, to extinguish the fire of the conflict that has broken out, while the timid women, paralyzed by shock, watch that breach of peace <note>original: *vredebreuk*; a term for the disruption of communal harmony</note> from a distance, weeping. Just as when a fire breaks out in a neighborhood and spreads through the granaries <note>original: *korenshuuren*; barns used for storing grain</note> at night, flickering and bright, the people run together, driven by the dismal cries; there, some throw themselves into the heat of danger, and others snatch away what can be saved; one pours water into the leaping flame to halt the all-consuming fire, until, preventing further disaster, one sees all that glow vanish into smoke.

<margin>Meanwhile, Abraham practices his solitary worship.</margin>
The Patriarch <note>Abraham, the biblical forefather</note>, long accustomed to seeking the lonely woods before the morning sun adorns herself in the ocean—her golden mirror—and arranges her crest of rays; there he praises and thanks the Divinity, and many times loses himself in the sea of his own contemplation: (Whether he chooses God’s Essence as his subject, gazing with fixed attention upon that perfection; or whether he lets his spirit sail through the great Universe <note>original: *Heelal*</note>, soaring through a thousand arches and countless circles; or dwells in contemplation of the beautiful globe of the earth, where a thousand signs of infinite Omnipotence <note>original: *Alvermogen*</note> reveal themselves in all creatures before his eyes:)

In our <term>Seed</term> <note>original: *Zade*; referring to the promised descendants of Abraham</note>, through faith:
An incomprehensible salvation, to be expected here above!"
At this, the Man falls silent, enraptured by holy thoughts.

<margin>The birth of war.</margin>
But meanwhile, the anxious rumor of war runs
Through all the cities of the land, and fills the air
With grim tidings from the army of the great
Chedorlaomer <note>original: *Laömer Kedor*; the King of Elam mentioned in Genesis 14</note> with his proud allies.
War, a foul monster, born of pride and luxury,
Now spawned in the voluptuous valley of Siddim,
And in the horrors of completely corrupted morals
Cherished and fed by five accursed cities:
A terrible monster that satisfies its hunger with gold,
And slakes its thirst with purple human blood,
And lives in rebellion, amidst murder and burning;
It now strikes its cursed torch into the entrails of the Princes.

<margin>At the time of the rebellion of the five cities in the valley of Siddim</margin>
The five Kings, who ruled in a fivefold
Of cities, in the beautiful and fertile valley of Siddim,
Had for twelve years <note>original: *zes paar jaren*, literally "six pair of years"</note> paid grain
And taxes to the Prince of Elam, and sworn homage and loyalty:
For just as many years, according to the old reports of the land,
Was wanton mother Luxury also pregnant with that fruit;
But in the following year, when Pride served the princes
As counselor, to cast off the yoke that their shoulders bore,

The raging Imperial Prince has war proclaimed.

The weaponry is forged and made sharp of edge.
A trio of Princes prepares for the march,

### THE FOURTH BOOK.

When Elam’s King, with his fame-bound hosts,

<margin>His victory.</margin>

It comes to Kadesh; strikes all who dare to resist,
In the land of Amalek; indeed, the courageous people spared
No Amorite <note>original: *Emoryt*</note>, who dwelt in Hazezon Tamar <note>An ancient name for En-gedi, a lush oasis near the Dead Sea.</note>.
Like a stream, which swells from melted mountain snow,

And foams through the fields, destroying the ears of grain,
And floods both house and yard with swift waves:
So the army of the four Victors <note>The four allied kings: Chedorlaomer, Amraphel, Arioch, and Tidal.</note> marches everywhere,
Through the surrounding land, into the fertile Valley of Siddim,
Eager for plunder, to break into its five cities,
And to avenge the King of Elam for his insult and shame.

O Heaven! Do you permit this? Righteous Supreme Lord!
<margin>Prays for him.</margin> Awake, and strike the barbarian down with your lightning!

From the clouds, for the salvation of your servant.
<margin>Is ignited by a heavenly heroic fire,</margin> Thus he prays, and his face begins to glow
With noble fire, like a burning garnet.

His gray hairs stand up on end <note>The Dutch phrase "te bergen opgerezen" suggests hair standing up in a moment of intense spiritual or emotional arousal, like a mountain peak.</note>:

### THE FIFTH BOOK.

<meta>catchword: Now</meta>

Betraying their debtor, they must pay for their injustice:
They shall descend alive into the abyss,
For wickedness and deceit guard their gates.
Come now, my friends, God Himself brings forth weapons for me.

<margin>His face is illuminated.</margin>
The heavenly heroic fire, ignited in Abram's breast,
Illuminates his face, after he had spoken,
More and more: just as the sun appears much more beautiful
When it smooths the wrinkles from the radiant face of nature,
Breaks through the water-clouds, and draws back the curtains of the air
From the eastern horizons, to shine upon the earth.
A noble wrath inspires him with strength and courage.
He is like a horse that neighs at the sound
Of the war-horn, foams, snorts, and shakes its mane,
And with its hooves scrapes the dust from the paths of the army camp. <note>This description of a war-horse echoes the famous passage in the biblical Book of Job (39:19-25), often used in epic poetry to signify martial readiness.</note>

<margin>Prayer of the Poet, for assistance in his work.</margin>
But grant, O Heaven! now to my <term>biblical poetry</term> <note>original: "bybelpoëzy"</note>
More flair and life, and to my spirit more strength;
O <term>Supreme Artist</term> <note>The poet addresses God as the ultimate creator and source of inspiration, a common trope in Christian epic poetry where the "Muse" is replaced by the Holy Spirit or the Creator.</note>, stretch my strings tighter!
Elaborate upon the concise style of <term>Moses</term>, your Scribe,
Graciously in my Poem, and teach me how your Friend,
The hero Abram, through your help, defeats four Kings;
And returns in triumph, laden with rich booty,
Greeted and blessed by the Princes for his deeds!
You remember the heroic feat; we have only heard of it,
Or see it, though in the distance, drawn in your word,
Where all the circumstances, even for the most attentive eyes,
Are covered with the dark mists of <term>Antiquity</term>! <note>original: "Alöudheit." The poet acknowledges that while the Bible provides the truth, the specific historical details of Abraham's era are lost to time.</note>

# ABRAHAM THE PATRIARCH.

<margin>Abram arms his servants, 318 in number.</margin>
Now everyone in the army camp is on their feet.
Hero Abram calls his people together for inspection,
His well-trained, his <term>home-born servants</term> <note>original: "ingeboren' knechten"; these were servants born into the household rather than purchased, often implying a deeper bond of loyalty to the patriarch.</note>,
All brave souls, strong of arm, ready to fight.
Three hundred men, and eighteen more in a line,
He girds with weapons and hangs the sword at their side:
To some he gives spears, tipped with steel at the point;
And to these axes, which they carry upon the shoulder;
And others show off with the resilient war-bow.
The shepherd's staff is now cast out of sight;
The <term>plowshare</term> <note>original: "kouter"; the sharp blade of a plow. This imagery evokes a reversal of the biblical prophecy of turning "swords into plowshares."</note> lies in the barn. The proud young men
Look upon the weaponry with desire, and feel their hearts leap:
One greases the armor, freshly forged in Hebron,
With fat, against the red rust that eats steel and iron;
This one drills with his spear: some test the arrows:
Some whet the hard battle-axes upon the stone;
Just as the mower, when the golden-yellow grain ripens,
Sharpens the curved sickle upon the hard whetstone.

<margin>Sarah’s concern and complaints over Abram's plan.</margin>
But chaste Sarah, moved by great concern,
Watched all these preparations with tears in her eyes,
And sighed at every command that Father Abram gave.
At last she says: "Oh, please abandon this plan,
My Lord, this fire of zeal exceeds your strength,
And seems to numb your great wisdom and reason.
Do you wish to defeat a conquering army (alas! what possesses you?)
With a weak band of shepherds?"

Will you strip four Kings of their rich prey?
Alas! Their overwhelming power will utterly crush you;
Felling you in an instant into the mire,
And I, miserable woman, shall not even behold
Your dear corpse, nor share in the funeral
With my floods of tears, to wash your mortal wounds!
Oh Heaven! Who then shall help me, a poor widow,
In that time of need, or comfort me in my mourning;
Since I, separated from friend, kin, and fatherland,
Wander miserably with my cattle in foreign meadows?
Have pity, Abram, ah! Where is the faithful love
That burned in your breast for me, your <term>heart’s companion</term> <note>original: "Hartvriendin"; a term of deep endearment, literally "heart-friend."</note>,
When the <term>Prince of the Nile</term> <note>original: "Nylvorst"; referring to the Pharaoh of Egypt who took Sarah into his harem in Genesis 12.</note> abducted me and brought me among courtly women?
Where is your wisdom, your faith, and 그at trust
In God’s Providence, with which you once awaited
The help of the Most High in silence within your humble shepherd’s hut?
The same God still lives, and His formidable power:
He who delivered me can also lift up our brother Lot.
I admit, I am grieved by the suffering of our <term>blood-relative</term> <note>original: "Bloetverwant"; here referring to Abram's nephew, Lot.</note>:
But who can move <term>Ebal’s peak</term> <note>original: "Ebals kruin"; Mount Ebal is a prominent, rocky mountain in Samaria, used here as a metaphor for something immovable and daunting.</note>? What hand

When a night-storm rages and lashes all the waves?
As little as a dove can rescue prey from the hawk’s claw:
As little as a woolly sheep can save a tender suckling lamb
From the mouths of wolves in the leafy forests:
So little can you deliver Lot from the enemy’s army.

If I desire a single thread, or even a lowly shoelace, more
Than the simple freedom of my blood-relative <note>Abram is referencing the oath he swore to God (Genesis 14:22-23), refusing to take any spoils of war for himself so that none could say they made him rich.</note>;

<margin>And rest until the night.</margin>
Then I shall rejoice in your salvation, and raise
The war banner to your honor by the grateful altar-fire.
Thus spoke the Hero, and granted rest to his servants,
In order to combat the enemy in the dark of night.

<margin>Drunkenness enters Kedor's camp.</margin>
But meanwhile, wicked Wantonness <note>original: "Dartelheit"; refers to a state of reckless frivolity or excessive playfulness that leads to sin.</note>
Had steadily flattered the hearts of the Princes in their prosperity,
And now sent Drunkenness into Kedor's <note>Kedor refers to Chedorlaomer, King of Elam.</note> army;
A godless vice, which—too evil and unrestrained—
Creeps upon the human spirit in the guise of merriment,
And slinks with the wine into one's lustful veins,
Extinguishing the fire of life, weakening the strength of the nerves,
Bewildering and numbing the senses and thoughts;
A dread beast that destroys hope, murders virtue,
And smothers the noblest gifts in the lap of lust;
It breaks God’s order, violates nature, and darkens
Noble reason in a vapor of shame and horrors;
<margin>Whose nature and properties are described.</margin>
A strife-harpy <note>A mythological reference used here to personify Drunkenness as a creature that sows discord.</note> who ignites the quiet mind into anger,
Who takes from Temperance her bridle, and from Plenty his horn,
Who has often oppressed modesty and chastity,
And tore all bonds of love to shreds:
How often has she not toppled Prosperity from her throne,
And placed Poverty in her stead, to her lasting shame,

### BOOK FIVE.

<sig>P</sig>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

And a disgrace to radiant honor! How often the features
Of the Divine Image, still faintly detectable in the human spirit,
The seeds of truth, virtue, and wisdom, are wickedly trampled!
Passions, lusts, and vile desires
Are nurtured, and man, with uneven steps,
Is led on display, clothed in beastly characteristics!
Thus a field is ruined by a constant pool of rain,
Spoiled to its very depths; and the earth, which was fertile,
Turns to wet mire, and all the furrows are closed up;
The noble seed rots before it has taken root,
And the husbandman <note>A traditional term for a farmer or someone who cultivates the land.</note>, who had imagined a fruitful harvest,
Then sees only a mud-puddle that breeds venomous vipers.

<margin>Chedorlaomer feasts the Kings and great Lords.</margin>
That wicked Drunkenness, the mother of misery,
Who surprised father Noah himself <note>A reference to the biblical story in Genesis 9, where Noah, the survivor of the Flood, was caught unaware by the potency of the wine from his first vineyard.</note> before he knew her,
Now saunters unhindered into the army tents,
Accompanied by Carelessness, her sworn companion.
The Prince Laomer <note>Chedorlaomer, the King of Elam and leader of the four-king alliance.</note> had his brave army commanders,
The warlike Kings, his bold allies,
Invited to his table, so that, during the festival time,
They might give free rein to joy, amidst wine and delicacies,
And celebrate without fear: fifty servants were seen there
Decorating the table with countless dishes,
And fifty cupbearers, standing around the table.
The Princes reclined upon the soft couches,
Covered with purple; the Lords sat on carpets
Of lesser value, according to the rank of the Elamites <note>Elam was an ancient civilization located in what is now southwestern Iran; in this epic, they are the primary antagonists.</note>.

Wanton Luxury, together with shameful Excess,
Trample underfoot the golden mean <note>The "golden mean" (referencing the Latin *aurea mediocritas*) is the classical ideal of moderation, avoiding all extremes.</note>
In the King’s tent, and cause, amidst the gorging and the drinking,
Wanton lust to shine upon their glowing cheeks.
<margin>Where Drunkenness plays her role,</margin>
The wine of Siddim <note>The Valley of Siddim, the site of the recent battle, was known for its richness and fertility before the destruction of the cities of the plain.</note>, which defies all other wines,
Foams in the goblet, and banishes care from the heart.
The golden drinking-cup is poured full four times over,
And four times drained to the health of the Sovereigns.
Each man pours, and vies to spill the precious liquid,
Which often splashes over the floor and the table.
They feast there on the spoils. The cheering rings to the clouds,
Celebrating the total defeat of the conquered nations:
One boasts of his deeds on the victorious march,
Another brags of his booty, brought back from Sodom,
And others know how to trace their lineage, through ancient signs,
Even back to Nimrod the Mighty <note>Nimrod, a descendant of Noah mentioned in Genesis, was traditionally considered the first powerful king and the builder of the Tower of Babel.</note>.
The entire army now also celebrates, at the royal command,
This festival, rejoicing with dance and the playing of strings.
The leather wine-skin slakes the thirst of the soldiers,
<margin>And Carelessness relieves the sentries.</margin>
With soldiers' gulps, until they reel on unsteady feet;
And various gestures and many disorders,
According to each man's own nature, are mingled together;
While Carelessness surely relieves the army guards
Everywhere from their posts, and wards off all second thoughts.

<margin>Abram divides his forces,</margin>
And evening hastened to spread the dark carpet,
And to lead a black night up toward the heavens;
While the Hero Abram steadily divides his army,
And commends loyalty and bravery to his servants.
Prince Aner must march through the forest with a hundred men,
And Eshcol with his people shall cover his brother’s attack.
The proud Mamre shall strike the left wing,
And march through the mountains against Tidal’s <note>Tidal, referred to in the Bible as the "King of Goiim," was one of the four kings allied with Chedorlaomer.</note> tents;
While my Abram himself, with his shepherd-servants,
Shall battle the great army of Chedorlaomer <note>Chedorlaomer (Laömer in the Dutch text), the King of Elam, was the leader of the Mesopotamian alliance that had plundered Sodom and Gomorrah.</note>.

<margin>And surprises the enemy in the night.</margin>
Thus they march forth, and fall, in the middle of the night,
Upon the enemy's flank—an enemy who now expects no foe:
They find everywhere the drunken camp-followers,
Lying here in the grass, there sleeping in the tent,
Where the lamp already burns with faint rays.
This one has the drinking cup, that one the musical instrument in hand,
And others hum, at the end of their strength,
The soft lullaby still with half-closed eyes;
But are, by the steel—after so much luxury and pleasure—
Lulled into the iron sleep <note>The "iron sleep" (*yzren slaap*) is a classical poetic metaphor for death, emphasizing its cold, unbreakable nature compared to the soft sleep of the living.</note> before they know it,
And their drowsy eyes are closed for eternity.
The sword drinks purple blood; so far do the comrades-in-arms
Overwhelm them; and Death runs with violence,
In and out of the tents, clattering across the field,
And strikes off the heads of the snoring war-bands,
Like a reaper in the harvest strikes the ears of corn.

Is on the tongues of the cheering populace,
And is highly praised by the joyful Canaanites;
While envy, formerly swelling in the hearts of the Perizzites <note>A group of people living in ancient Canaan alongside the Canaanites.</note>,
Consumes itself upon hearing his name;
But because the Princes themselves, moved by his fame,
Are already on their way to greet Abram:
Where the Kings of the land even meet the Shepherd <note>Abraham is often referred to as a "shepherd-prince," highlighting the contrast between his humble lifestyle and his martial power.</note>.
Thus wondrously does the Heavenly Majesty,
Through His governance, often exalt low humility,
And makes the Princes, swollen with pride,
Roll down to the earth from the precarious peak of power.

<margin>Abram arrives in the Valley of Shaveh,</margin>
Fertile Shaveh lies in Canaan's prospect,
A green meadow, where one sees neither tree nor hill,
A spacious plain, which, intersected with brooks,
Embroidered with flowers, was previously called
The Valley of Delights <note>Also known as the King's Dale; the traditional meeting place of Abram and the King of Sodom.</note>: here comes my Hero, from the defeat
Of the great enemy, with his triumphal procession,
And sees in the open valley from afar two brown clouds
Of dust rising, from the approach of two peoples:
Just as the south wind sometimes creates a mist
On the high mountains, and covers their lofty peaks:
So the dust cloud also covers the peoples before his eyes.
The prince of Sodom comes drawn to the left side,
The young Bera, who only recently, with the crown
Of Sodom on his head, ascended his father’s throne:

### THE FIFTH BOOK.
<sig>Q 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<margin>Where the King of Sodom comes to meet Abram.</margin>
He mounted his royal chariot as the rumor of Chedorlaomer’s <note>Original: "Kedor." This refers to Chedorlaomer, the Elamite king who had previously asserted dominance over the region.</note> defeat was carried through the Five-Cities <note>Original: "Vyfstat." This refers to the Pentapolis: Sodom, Gomorrah, Admah, Zeboiim, and Bela.</note>, followed by nobles and envoys, not only from Sodom, but from all five plundered cities. They came to meet the Victor over the tyrannical overlords, who now ensured that his father’s blood did not lack for vengeance (for the old Prince, before he died of his wound, had personally charged him with this revenge). He sought to meet Abram on his way from such a glorious campaign, and to greet him reverently in his victory. From the other side came the godly man, Prince Melchizedek, the honor and glory of Canaan,
<margin>Also Melchizedek, the King of Salem.</margin>
that great King of righteousness and peace: he, who upon Salem’s <note>Original: "Zalem." Traditionally identified with Jerusalem; the name implies "Peace."</note> throne also held the priesthood of the Most High God; a wonder of his time. In Heaven’s high Counsel, he was consecrated as a prototype of the World’s Savior, who, according to his manner and order, would become a Prince and Priest forever, according to God’s sworn oath. That King, untainted by the idolatries of those corrupt times, departs from the City of Peace <note>Referring again to Salem.</note>, and comes, with all the venerable men of his High Council and those who preside over the tribunal, followed quite stately by his sacrificial procession. He comes to meet the victory-parade of my Abram, to piously encounter this Hero of God,
<margin>To bless Abram and his hon[or]—</margin>
and to bless him as a Priest of the Most High God.

Delivered by your arm, would tell of your deeds,

<margin>Abram gives the tithes to Melchizedek.</margin>

Which without envy shall see you as the possessor
Of all the treasures, by the raging men of war

According to the right of war, have acquired in ownership,

# ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.

<meta>catchword: This</meta>

Take all the plunder for yourself, the property and all the cattle;
But grant, as a pledge of friendship and of peace,
To us the souls. Release the men and the women
(Whom you might otherwise lawfully keep in your servitude)
From their slavery: enjoy the treasure and the goods;
But grant, oh brave Hero, the Siddimite his blood <note>The "Siddimmyt" refers to the inhabitants of the Valley of Siddim; "his blood" is a poetic reference to the lives of his people.</note>.

<margin>Abram’s answer to the King of Sodom.</margin>
But godly Abram, driven by no desire for glory,
As noble-minded as he is pious in his life,
Says: no, oh King, I do not desire your treasures:
I have my hands, before the attack had even occurred,
Raised upward to God, who in the clouds
Has His dwelling, and rules the earth and all its peoples,
And there sworn before His all-seeing Eye,
That I went to war for no robbery or booty:
I began the war only for Lot, my nephew,
And, through God’s assistance, defeated his plunderer:
I desire no treasure that belongs to you, oh Sovereign;
Not a thread, not a sandal-strap <note>original: "geen' draat, geen' voetzoolriem." This is a direct reference to Genesis 14:23, emphasizing Abram's refusal to profit from the rescue mission.</note>; I keep my word to God:
I wish to enjoy nothing from the cattle of the Siddimites;
So that you might never, though unjustly, reproach me
Or be able to say: I have made Abram rich:
He obtained his wealth through robbery and plunder.
No, no, the Divinity has already let Her mild blessing
Descend upon me, like a balmy summer rain,
And has added to me treasures, salvation, and prosperity.
God makes me rich, and I am satisfied with that wealth.

<term>Siddimmyt</term> — An inhabitant of the Valley of Siddim, the location of Sodom and Gomorrah.
<term>Ryksvorſt</term> — A sovereign prince or ruler of a realm.
<term>Godtheit</term> — The Divinity or Godhead.

<summary>The King of Sodom offers Abram all the recovered goods if he will only return the captured people, but Abram refuse any personal gain, citing an oath made to God to ensure no man can claim credit for his wealth.</summary>

## THE FIFTH BOOK.
<meta>catchword: But</meta>

Therefore this remains far from me: I only wish to stipulate,
O Prince, repayment for all that my young men
Consumed on the journey; the costs are not great:
In this way, I already treat you as an Ally <term>Ally: original "Bontgenoot," referring to a formal military partner or confederate.</term>.
But as for the portion of these brave men,
My Allies, who joined their forces with my army,
The valiant Aner, Mamre, and Eshcol: let them freely
Retain their right to the spoils; that is not for me to decide.

<margin>General joy in the Valley of Shaveh.</margin>
Now the joyful cheering rises to the very vaults of heaven.
The green meadow itself seems to leap and dance,
Proclaiming the freedom of the Siddimites <note>Siddimites: The inhabitants of the Valley of Siddim, who had been taken captive by the four northern kings.</note>,
Who cast off and break their chains and bonds
At the sound of such a joyous clamor,
To stretch out free hands toward their fellow countrymen;
They embrace friend and kin, and, full of joy, they praise
The Commander Abram, and his wondrous skill in war.
Now happiness has filled the bowls to the brim,
Refreshing the hearts of the weary companions
With royal wine. Everyone is on their feet.
The crowds now swarm and mingle together,
Like ants on hot summer days.
The plunder is gathered, and one sees them lugging and carrying,
The dividing of the spoils, the treasures, and the cattle.
A noise is heard there, like the roaring of the sea
When the wind, exhausted from wrestling with the waves,
Falls into a slumber, and allows the sky to clear once more.

### ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.

Or row past all danger, through your hand!
Come then: we hasten forth; the following wind stretches
The sails; my ship surges through the waves of letters,
And gives us hope to one day sail into the harbor.

<margin>Ten years after Abraham's departure from Ur of the Chaldeans,</margin>
The joyful spring had the earth, far and wide,
The trees and all the fields ten times anew clothed,
Adorned with flowers, and made to show gloriously;
And ten times had the autumn, drunk with must <note>New wine or unfermented grape juice.</note> and grapes,
Torn the green wigs <note>A poetic Dutch metaphor for the leafy canopies of trees.</note> from the forests,
Trampled the carpet of the fields, pressed down the flowers:
Since Father Abraham, separated from his fatherland,
Drove his cattle into the fertile pastures of Canaan:
When Lady Sarah, tormented by worry,
Went walking through the field with her Lord:
She sighed, at the sight of all the activity of the servants,
Of all the wealth of their cattle and sheep,

<margin>Sarah became distressed over her infertility,</margin>
And spoke: how richly has mild Fruitfulness
Extended her fragrant hands, through God's blessing!
How has she not blessed our house, and how the flock!
It is as if it had rained sheep in the valleys;
And as if this * Ogis <note>A name for the region near Mamre, derived from ancient sources.</note> had grown into a city
Of shepherd tents, where rich prosperity blooms.
But alas! what use can all those blessings be to us,
If through death we must leave them to a stranger?

## THE SIXTH BOOK.

->R 2<-

What use is it that one plows, or plants tree or vineyard,
If a stranger should pluck the fruit with his hand?
For Sarah, Sarah alone must live barrenly,
And can give no heir to you, my Abram.
The birds breed in the treetops in the May season,
The fish in the stream, the cattle in the youthful green;
The fertile <note>Original: *teelzieke*, a vivid seventeenth-century Dutch word meaning "breeding-sick" or intensely procreative.</note> earth opens her pregnant womb constantly,
To heap us with fruits and with riches:
But Sarah alone remains deprived of fruitfulness,
<margin>And laments this fact;</margin>
And has not yet borne her Abram any fruit of marriage.
The servants, married to maidens and slave-girls,
Increase our house: they breed and produce
Young boys for us: the flock lambs constantly:
No cow miscarries; the goat gives birth in the thorn hedge;
But Sarah may not satisfy herself with any true offspring.
Why must you, O Abram, still plow the barren shores,
Sowing your grain in the infertile wilderness?
Do not expect the Seed <note>Referring to the "Seed of the Promise," the prophesied descendant through whom God promised to bless all nations.</note> of God’s promise from me:
But let me, my Lord, guide you for once by my counsel,
While the procreative power still plays in your firm muscles:
I have a young maiden, so beautiful of face,
<margin>And offers Abram her handmaid Hagar, so that he might be built up <note>The Dutch *gebout* (built up) refers to the ancient legal and cultural concept of a wife "building a house" or family through a surrogate.</note> through her.</margin>
And fresh of limb, as she is faithful in her duty;
The fair Hagar, whom we bought in Egypt;
The joy of the young men, who already sought to love her!
Take her alongside me as a wife, on my advice:
So we shall see from her womb a son, a pleasant seed

### ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.
<meta>catchword: Then</meta>

"Through Abram, who shall build our house and your lineage,
And whom I myself intend to keep as my own son;
Since the Almighty, the Giver of the fruit of the body,
Who holds the key to the dark womb,
In all the wealth we have enjoyed for so long a time,
Has closed my body, like an earthen vessel."

<margin>*Abram is astonished by this proposal.*</margin>
Thus spoke Lady Sarah, and the great Shepherd <note>The "Great Shepherd" refers to the Patriarch Abraham, reflecting his role as both a leader of people and a literal keeper of vast flocks.</note> stood
Amazed at what he heard from her lips.
His solemn expression reveals the depth of his thoughts.
Who would ever expect such a proposal from a woman!
(Thus he spoke) "No, my love, my Sarah, have patience;
God’s holy promise <note>Original: *heilbelofte*. This refers specifically to the Covenant in which God promised Abraham a son and numerous descendants.</note> shall surely be fulfilled in you.
He created but one woman in fair and fertile Eden
For Adam, though custom has since violated that law."

<margin>*Tells Sarah of his latest vision.*</margin>
"But listen to what has happened to me recently, what I saw
And heard, as I lay there in deep melancholy:
I saw a wondrous light descend upon our tent;
I saw a vision that no tongue can describe,
And heard a voice, God’s voice, which stills my sorrow thus:
'Fear not, O Abram, for I am your shield;
I am your salvation, your reward, in life and in death.'
Then I cried: 'Lord! O Lord! what shall I truly gain?
Since the time of my life surely passes without a descendant <note>Original: *Nazaat*. Referring to a biological heir.</note>;
My steward from Damascus <note>A reference to Eliezer of Damascus (Genesis 15:2), whom Abraham initially feared would be his only heir.</note> alone shall
Inherit my riches when my life is at its end;
I have no one else; to me You have given no son.'"

Then the Almighty spoke, clothed in sunshine:

As soon as the morning greeted me with its rays,

In all the features of that pious face.
Then the Divine Voice cried: he upon whom you cast your eyes,
Shall be the greatest of all, an instrument in my hands,
Through whom I shall work wonders in neighboring lands:
He shall, hated, sold, and led into slavery,
From dark dungeons to a high dominion
Be raised up, and thus serve as an example
Of the great Savior <note>The author uses the story of Joseph as a "type" or foreshadowing of Jesus Christ—both were betrayed by their own and later became saviors of their people.</note>, to be brought forth after many centuries:
Men shall bow down before him because of a great famine.
<margin>And the same, after the death of Joseph <note>original: "Joſef"</note>, foretells oppression:</margin>
But know, O Abram, that your seed, after his death,
Shall be oppressed and held in bondage in that foreign land <note>This refers to the Israelites' four-century-long stay in Egypt.</note>,
By cruel nations, for four hundred years from now;
But the nation that makes your lineage bow under oppression,
I shall also judge, and strike with heavy blows <note>A reference to the Ten Plagues of Egypt.</note>:
Then shall your great lineage from its harsh masters
Depart with their possessions, and finally return
To Canaan <note>original: "Kanaän"</note>, when to the fourth generation
The ancestral inheritance shall be delivered;
<margin>Regarding the timeframe for the inheritance of the land of Canaan,</margin>
For the iniquity of the local peoples <note>This refers to the biblical concept that the Canaanites (Amorites) would not be displaced until their sins had reached a "full measure" (Genesis 15:16).</note>
Does not yet draw down my stern vengeance from the clouds.
But you, O Abram, shall with peace in your soul
Go to your fathers, and without misfortune,
In a good old age, descend to the dark grave.
Then I lifted my eyes, while the sunbeams
Faded more and more before the evening twilight:
But when it was dark, I saw, O strange Vision!

A smoking oven, and a fiery torch burning,

From where the Shihor <note>original: "Sichor"; a biblical name for a branch of the Nile or the "Brook of Egypt," marking the traditional southwestern border of the Promised Land.</note>, by Egypt, divides the land;

<margin>Sarah answers and persists.</margin> At this point the hero Abram grew silent, and Lady Sarah took up the word.
No unbelief, my lord, has ever spurred me on.
How! should I doubt God’s promises?
O no. The sun shall sooner miss its annual course,

Sooner shall the waters of the flowing Jordan

Than God’s promise shall fail to be the truth.
I believe that He shall give you a descendant,
From your own body, for His decree stands firm;
But has the Divinity—who has already several times
Descended from His heavenly throne to this earth,
Who spoke with you as a man speaks with his friend—
Ever assured you that you would obtain that promised child,
The seed which, according to His word, shall inherit this Canaan,
Through your Sarah, your barren wife?

### THE SIXTH BOOK.
S
<meta>catchword: God</meta>

<margin>
*She persists with Abram regarding a second marriage.*
</margin>
Oh no. Oh Abram, you do not understand your freedom.
Where is the law that forbids you a second wife?
How can you ever transgress where no law exists?
Though God created only one woman in Eden,
That established no marriage law for all mankind:
Men have often chosen a second wife within our lineage;
But to whom was it ever imputed as a vice?
Come now: oh, surrender yourself to reason.
If the All-Blesser <note>original: "Alzegenaar"; a poetic title for God as the source of all blessings.</note> has promised you a rich harvest,
And you see yourself year after year deprived of nourishing grain,
Your hope disappointed? Then sow your fertile seed
On a better field, in the freshly plowed furrows,
Since all the fertile land is nonetheless in your power;
It is in vain if you expect an heir from me.
If the Nile <note>original: "Nylstroom"</note> did not overflow in its season,
To water the thirsty fields with its fertile sap,
And all the springs, which flow through a thousand veins,
Dried up, how then would the farmer see any fruit?
Or would he wait idly for God's miracles?
Oh no, my Abram, repel those vain thoughts:
If one builds one's faith solely upon heavenly wonders;
Trusting all too recklessly in an obscure matter,
And, without using the means at hand, continues to hope for the Most High's help,
Then one might easily wander from the path of virtue:
Then piety becomes filled with superstition,
And our understanding is deaf to the sound of reason.

<summary>Sarah argues that Abram is not legally or morally forbidden from taking a second wife, using agricultural metaphors to suggest that human action and "reason" must assist in fulfilling divine promises.</summary>

Thus spoke Lady Sarah; and the Hero Abram, whom before

But the deferred hope for the long-promised seed!"

### ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.

But is it any wonder that a painful resentment

And creeps into her heart, forcing tears from her eyes?
Oh no: virtue can never tolerate ingratitude,
But hates that monster, which surpasses all other vices.
The scorn of one who elevates herself after receiving a kindness
Falls hard, making the pressure and sorrow rise even higher.
An oak tree itself stands distressed, appearing to feel,
And mourns when the ivy <note>original: "klim"; ivy is used here as a classic literary metaphor for a parasitic person who thrives by clinging to a stronger host, eventually choking it.</note> clings to its branches,
Yes, even weaving its wanton vines around the tree's very crown,
And all too ungratefully robs it of its freedom, bloom, and life,
After the trunk had first given it support,
And lifted it up from the shifting sands in its tender youth.
Thus Lady Sarah likewise pines from pure resentment, and mourns,
Until she bursts forth, and complains of the insult to Abram:

Dares to mock me for my infertility—as if it were in our power!—
While it, alas! before both my eyes...”

Is it thus that I stand upon a sea of tears, year after year?
I rescued her from servitude, and it is true,

But now that she is pregnant—and should have given me that joy—
I am despised by her, and have become an object of her scorn,
And mockery in the household management, which she now seeks to control;
Indeed, if she continues in these high-flown thoughts,
I shall soon have slavery to look forward to as my lot:

Or else all the injustice and insult I suffer shall fall upon you:
Otherwise, God shall justly pass sentence,
And set Himself as a Judge between you and me.
Thus spoke Lady Sarah with tears upon her face;
<margin>He comforts her.</margin>

# ABRAHAM THE PATRIARCH.

<margin>Sarah attempts in vain to humble Hagar,</margin>

Ruining and flooding the hopes of the countryfolk:
Thus pride cannot be restrained by reason

<margin>Who takes flight.</margin>

### ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.
<meta>catchword: Up-</meta>

To behold My Glory, as if in a mirror,
And to unfold the deep secret of my mysteries:
Thus must, O Eternal Word—who once so skillfully
Fashioned this throne, when the Spirit of my mouth
Created all the host of heaven—the seed of Abraham
<margin>That the history of Abraham must also be thus,</margin>
Show great things to your people in its reflections.
The servant-son, who is in the womb of Hagar,
Conceived of Abraham according to the flesh, shall surely
Serve as a symbol for all the children
That Abraham's fleshly seed shall multiply without number.
The handmaid Hagar is the subservient Sinai, <note>A reference to the biblical allegory in Galatians 4, where Mount Sinai (representing the Mosaic Law) is compared to the enslaved Hagar.</note>

As soon as You, O Sun of Salvation, shall appear before mankind:
<margin>And what is to be symbolized by Sarah and Hagar with their sons.</margin>
But free Sarah shall represent the new covenant,
Which pours out my grace and salvation upon the world’s circle;
The free state of the church, removed from the subservient yoke,
Which shall see your radiance with unclouded eyes.
From Sarah shall soon be begotten the blessed seed,
The free Son of my promise, the Symbol <term>Zinnebeelt: In this context, a 'type' or prophetic symbol where an Old Testament figure foreshadows a New Testament reality.</term>

Knows my mysteries in later ages,

Fastened to pillars in the spiritual building of the church, <note>original: "kerkgebou" — the poet envisions the history of the patriarchs as a series of grand paintings or tapestries hung within the metaphorical architecture of the Christian Church.</note>

O yes, my eyes and my hearing are witnesses,

But you, O Water-spring, shall witness this forever:

<margin>She immortalizes the water-well with the name Lahai Roi, and returns to Sarah.</margin>
You shall, as long as your waters flow through silver veins,

By the nations after this.
Thus she spoke, and rose up from the spring,
Which stood between Kadesh and the fertile Bered,
And turns back, and travels, until she found Abram's camp.

<margin>A portrayal of her conversion.</margin>
Thus can the almighty Word break hearts of stone,
When it begins to preach in the temple of the mind:
Then pride, delusion, and the thirst for vain honor,
With self-love, fall down like so many arrogant Dagons <note>Dagon was a Philistine deity whose idol, according to the Bible (1 Samuel 5), repeatedly fell face-down and broke before the Ark of the Covenant; the poet uses this as a metaphor for the humbling of human ego.</note>.

The pious Shepherd is, at the divine command,

By fire and sulfur, with four cities is overturned.

The wondrous Council of divine Attributes.

At an appointed time, to promise a son from Sarah;

The ghastly fire of vengeance already kindled, with beam upon beam,

Justice now gripped the overflowing horn
Of disasters in her fist, and Omnipotence stood ready.
The shrill trumpet of vengeance echoed three times: woe!
All the Angels sighed, so that a sigh rippled through the celestial heights. <note>original: "oppertranſen" — literally "upper battlements," referring here to the highest reaches of the heavens.</note>
The gemstones and the shining gold gave off sad-red glows,

Observe whether Sodom’s case is finally concluded;

### ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.

Since now my Abraham wears gray hairs?
For both were now old and advanced in years:
Indeed, nature itself had already withheld the signs:
It went no more as it goes with fruitful women.
The womb was already dead within her body.

But God, the All-Knower, who with all-seeing eyes

'Should I yet give birth in my old barrenness?
What! should anything be too great, too wondrous in the eyes,
Or impossible for the divine Omnipotence?
I shall return in this same season of the year,
And Sarah shall nourish a son from her breasts.'
But Mother Sarah, overcome with wonder,
Denies the matter, and speaks, moved by shame and fear:
I did not laugh, my Lord. But God, who sees everything,
Said once again: you did laugh, do not deny the truth.

<margin>Abraham escorts his guests out.</margin>
In that confusion the men have risen,
Whose heavenly origin shines ever more from their being;
They turn their faces toward Sodom, and depart;
While the ground blossoms with flowers wherever they tread,
And along the hills a heavenly scent spread abroad.
My faithful hero of God gives them a friendly escort,
And Sarah follows them with wondering eyes;
And cries: O strange event! who ever saw the like?

<margin>While Sarah, in wonder, feels the power of faith.</margin>
He prophesied, and knew all that I thought, oh wonder!

To impart the doctrine of the true faith to his children,
And recommend virtue and truth to his household;
That everyone might thus be led into the way of the Lord,

So that the All-Blesser from His azure halls <note>original: "azuure zalen" — a common poetic reference in this period to the blue sky or the heavens.</note>
May cause the salvation, promised to Abraham, to descend!

But Abraham perseveres in showing his love:
He girds himself with courage; yet in the recognition
That he is but dust and ashes before the Supreme Being;

In the throes of labor, to bring forth from her entrails
The matter, as a second cause <note>In seventeenth-century theology, God is the "First Cause," while natural elements like fire or storms are the "second causes" (secunda causa) He uses to enact His will.</note> in the hand of God.

</margin>
The volatile particles of saltpeter and sulfur,
Which the Eye of the World <note>A poetic metaphor for the sun.</note> from the earth’s dark navel

Now God’s Omnipotence, from the orbits and the depths

Now assemble, and, pushed close together,

Covering the sun, and spreading a twilight darkness

Through the terrifying flickering of the fire- and sulfur-rain,

Governing all things according to His will and decree;

<margin>Through which God rules the world,</margin>
She has the Almighty at her service, and watches the whole earth through the eyes

Fat riches, and lean poverty,
