Or justly pours out well-deserved plagues:
When it pleases Her, then fruitfulness sits on display,
In autumn's finest dress, upon a golden throne
Of ears of corn, and gladdens all the nations:
When it pleases Her, then thunder rides through the clouds;
Then lightning or hail blights the ripe grain,
Whenever mankind has sinned against the Deity.
That divine Power, now working specifically
At the court of Gerar, performs a miracle in the King’s house:
She who—just as when God created man from earth,
As well as when He fashioned the woman while Adam slept—
Had poured the blood and <term>geesten: the "vital spirits," which early modern thinkers believed were refined substances in the blood that carried life and thought</term> into the vessels,

<margin>Who also had created the body of man.</margin>
The wondrous parts, and well-ordered limbs,
The sensory organs, the heart, and all the entrails,
Had planted as a seat for human life,
And prescribed to every part its duties,
And given fixed laws of movement;
So that, when the Almighty first sent the soul into the body,
Gave life, and breathed the breath into the mouth,
The greatest Masterpiece of divine Omnipotence,
That <term>kleine Waerelt: "little world" or microcosm, the philosophical idea that the human body is a miniature map of the entire universe</term>, was moved by itself:

# THE EIGHTH BOOK

She, who ignited the fire of love in the first pair <note>Adam and Eve</note>,
And in those sparks, through the workings of nature,
Nurtured the seeds of countless future generations;
Now works a strange plague, through incomprehensible powers,

<margin>
*Now, for Sarah's sake, He closes all wombs in the King's house.*
</margin>
She withholds fertility, and seals the womb:
She binds the passion and desire, which lately flowed through the veins,
With a strange bond of pure impotence,
Through which love mourns, along with all luxury and lust;
And even prosperity is prematurely extinguished
In a cool pining, which no medicine can soothe;
Just as in a storm, when the wind begins to blow hard,
The cautious housewife shuts all the doors, fearing
Fire and calamity, and removes the fire from the hearth,
Extinguishes the coals, and hides and smothers the sparks:
So too does God’s Power extinguish the fire of the King’s wanton glances,
To preserve the honor of that peerless Woman <note>Sarah</note>,
Who would soon receive the long-promised Seed,
The Heir of God’s covenant, and His grace.
<margin>

</margin>
Meanwhile, the Sovereign of Gerar makes known to his Council
His marriage decree, in which everyone rejoices.
All the people, awakened by the loud rumor, leap for joy,
And run out curiously. One hears, through all the streets,
Talk of Sarah’s beauty, and of the wealthy Shepherd <note>Abraham, whom the King believes is merely Sarah's brother</note>,
Who had so many sheep, so many servants and slaves,
And everyone promises themselves prosperity and blessing for the city.

<meta>Continuing from page 185, the narrative moves from the general description of God’s intervention at Abimelech's court to the specific emotional and physical states of the characters. While the King and his men are struck with a physical "bond" or impotence to protect Sarah’s honor, Abraham and Sarah remain steadfast in their faith.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num>
<header>ABRAHAM, THE PATRIARCH.</header>
<meta>catchword: Re- (from Reassured)</meta>

<margin>*Abraham and Sarah are reassured by God’s governance.*</margin>
But Abraham is, by God’s spirit, from anxious fearing
Reassured, relying on the wise governance of the Eternal Being;
And Sarah feels in the court the power of holy faith:
There she, blind to glances, deaf to flatteries,
And unmoved in the grandly decorated halls,
Makes chaste modesty shine from her eyes.
The King was enraptured by that most beautiful face,
The whiteness of her cheeks, and the glowing rosy flesh-tones <note>original: "inkarnaat," a technical term for flesh-colored pigments in painting</note>,
The purple of her mouth, and the living jet of her eyes;
But he feels increasingly a pining impotence,
That lurks in his veins and halts his passion,
Locking his lust for luxury in unknown bonds,
And extinguishing his imagination and amorous thoughts.

<margin>*But God’s plague works within the men,*</margin>
Thus Pichol <note>The commander of Abimelech’s army</note> also lacked his usual manly strength.
Thus it goes in the court with the Prince’s household,
With all the men, however much they were flattered by women’s love.
The little kisses, laughs, and charming glances
Delighted the eye, indeed, and seemed to ignite the heart;
But neither sweet glance, nor laugh, nor kisses of the mouth;
Nor everything that love ever found tender and lovely,
With which it ever proved the power of that passion,
Could now cure the men of this strange plague:
Yes, even nature had no power over the pining body:
That bond was loosened: her dominion was at an end.

<margin>*And in the women a great*</margin>
The King’s wives, and illustrious Princesses,
The ladies-in-waiting, and married servants,

<summary>This page describes how God intervened at the court of King Abimelech by striking the men, including the King and his general Pichol, with a temporary impotence to protect Sarah. Despite being surrounded by the King's advances and the luxury of the palace, Sarah remains steadfast in her modest faith.</summary>

<margin>confusion in the court.</margin>
Feel as well God’s plague within their pregnant wombs:
Each shrinks from sorrow, or from hard labor-pains;
But no one gives birth, or is delivered from bitter suffering,
Through the help of the Sages, or through the power of herbal-medicines,
Thus Providence closed the fruitful womb.
She who was nursing, and just now her little son, happy in spirit,
Pressed to her full breast, driven by mother-love,
Can now give the crying babe no morning-nursing:
The pure source of milk is dried up, and in that pain
The whimpering of the infant goes to everyone’s heart.
Every person stands amazed, and the Doctors in particular.
Their art is in vain; nothing can prevail against the divine wonder.
The Priest calls in vain upon the deaf gods.
They make the men pass through the purifying-fires, <note>Ritual fires used in ancient ceremonies to cleanse the body of illness or spiritual impurity.</note>
And all the women wash themselves in holy water.

<margin>After eight days God comes to Abimelech in a dream.</margin>
For eight days long the entire court melted in pools of tears.
For eight days the man languishes, and the wife sits in distress,
And the infant cries from thirst upon the mother’s lap;
And for so many days Lady Sarah sees all the commotion
Of the Princely court, when the Almighty from heaven
Comes to Abimelech, by night, just before the light
Of the joyful dawn—for whom the stars give way—
Rises from the horizon; when the brain, through soft slumber,
Is refreshed with new spirits, and the imagination <note>original: "denkbeelt," referring to the early modern belief that dreams were physical impressions made upon the brain by "animal spirits."</note>
Makes a roomier and deeper impression in the vessels of the brain,
And says: "Now you are a dead man <note>original: "nu zyt gy om," a Dutch idiom implying death or ruin, following the phrasing of Genesis 20:3.</note> because of the Woman, whom you have abducted,"

And spoke: See here, my land lies free and open before you,
O Friend of God! Dwell wherever you please with your people;
But pray to the God whom I saw in the night-vision,
So that we may be free of His plague, and may live.
<margin>And admonishes Sarah.</margin> And you, whose fair face could compel me to love,
See, I give your Brother <note>original: "Broeder" — Abimelech refers to Abraham as Sarah's brother, following the couple’s strategic deception to avoid being killed for her beauty.</note> a thousand pieces of silver;
See to it that this serves you as a covering for the eyes <note>A biblical idiom referring to a gift meant to restore honor or signify Sarah's status as a married woman, shielding her from the advances of others.</note>,
That it may cover your beauty from the sight of strangers;
So that everyone, wherever you might graze the cattle,
May distinguish you from the maidens by your womanly headdress <note>original: "huifel" — a traditional hood or veil that denoted a woman’s married status.</note>,
And thus finally be taught by the misfortune of another.

<margin>Abraham offers a thank-offering.</margin> Thus God’s Providence has once again turned fear into joy.
So my Hero <note>The author frequently refers to Abraham as "my Hero" or "the Hero," casting the patriarch in an epic light.</note> triumphs, who, burdened with a blessing from the clouds,
And rich gifts from the King of these nations,
Brings his Sarah, his beloved heart-friend,
As if in triumph, from the court into the field-tents.
Now he has the sacrificial animal slaughtered in the shepherds' camp,
And thanks the Deity with reverent thoughts,
<margin>And prays for the King and his house;</margin> He prays for the King’s well-being, and for all the royal blood,
That must still, for his sake, suffer God’s plague;
And at his prayer, the plague is immediately bound.
Nature once again finds in the King and his Nobles
Its hands loose and free, and goes its old way.
<margin>Whereupon the plagues disappear.</margin> The procreative power <note>original: "teelkracht" — the biological force of fertility and reproduction.</note> flows through the blood in the veins without constraint,
The women give birth, and the fountains of milk spring forth
With life’s nourishment for the tender infants:

Just as, after a long drought, when the earth pants with thirst,
And stifles from the heat, sealing its bosom with a crust,
While the trees in the forest, and plant, and shrub alike,
Let their half-withered leaves hang despondently,
A gentle rain-shower drips from fruitful clouds,
And crawls again through the veins of the faded greenery,
Softening the ground, and opening the pregnant womb
Of the fertile Mother Earth, so that springs and brooks flow,
To the refreshment of herb, and grass, and cattle in the field,
Which restores the joy and hope of the husbandman.

<margin>God visits Sarah as he had spoken,</margin>
But the godly Couple had barely escaped
That final night of worry, when the friendly morning
Of God's promised favor shines, with a lovely light,
Through the long-deferred hope, upon Sarah’s face,
And works a miracle, such that nature and her powers,
Human ingenuity, and all its contemplations,
Are dimmed; and now He marks the pattern in that Woman
<margin>And makes her an example of the greatest Miracle.</margin>
Of the greatest Miracle, that the Godhead would work
In a pure Virgin <note>The author uses "typology" here—a traditional Christian interpretation where Old Testament events foreshadow the New Testament. Sarah’s miraculous pregnancy is seen as a "type" or precursor to the Virgin Birth of Jesus.</note>, who, against all her order
And the workings of nature, would be overshadowed
By the Almighty, and, impregnated by that Power,
Would bear God’s Son, the Salvation of the human race.
The Miracle-worker, having broken through the heavens,
Now visits Sarah, according to the word spoken long ago.
He, who closed the wombs of the King's wives <note>A reference to the previous chapter where God struck Abimelech’s household with infertility as punishment for taking Sarah.</note>,
Now grants fertility to a withered womb <note>original: "versturven schoot" — literally a "dead" or "perished" womb, referring to Sarah's post-menopausal state at age ninety.</note>,

Having been visited by God as promised, her vitality is restored, her pregnancy begins, and she eventually gives birth to Isaac, bringing joy to the entire household and the natural world.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>

<sig>Al</sig>
<meta>catchword: Al</meta>

At an age of ten times nine years,
She breathes a new youth through the blood, in heart and veins;
A secret fire, that tempers again the passion and strength,
Plays on Sarah’s cheeks, and beams from her eyes,
And invites her Lord anew to the wedding in her tent.
The gentle breeze that, at the arrival of spring,
Whistles open the deep-frozen womb of the earth,
Makes the brooks flow, and breathes into grass and herb
A new youth and fragrance; decorated by the gentle whispering,
The face of the earth is never adorned with more beautiful brilliance and luster;

<margin>Sarah becomes pregnant,</margin>
Than now God’s own Power paints upon the face
Of beautiful Sarah, who, illuminated by holy faith,
In her contemplation now sees the pure truth
Of the holy promises in her day and full clarity;

<margin>And bears a Son at the appointed time,</margin>
For she is pregnant, and bears for Abraham, her Head <note>In the biblical and historical context of this text, "Head" refers to the husband's status as the leader of the family unit.</note>,
At the appointed time, just as God had promised him,
By the oak grove of Mamre, in the shade of the leaves,
In his age of one hundred years of life,
A well-formed Son, whom she suckles at her breasts,
And thanks the Deity, who raises her out of disgrace <note>original: "smaat". In the ancient world, being unable to bear children was often viewed as a social reproach or a sign of divine disfavor. Sarah’s joy marks the end of this perceived shame.</note>.

<margin>General joy,</margin>
Now the joy spreads to the groups of shepherdesses
And the lads, where they graze the Lord’s flocks;
Each hums, and sings, or plays, in a shepherd’s way,
The praise of Sarah, and in honor of her young Son.
The feathered creatures break into song at all the cheering,
And the mute cattle begin to frolic and leap.

<summary>This page describes the miraculous rejuvenation and pregnancy of ninety-year-old Sarah, culminating in the birth of Isaac at the oak grove of Mamre. The event is celebrated as a divine miracle that fulfills God's promise, bringing immense joy to Abraham’s entire household and even the natural world.</summary>

# THE EIGHTH BOOK.

All the field, even nature itself, seems joyful of spirit:
But Elihu, a youth raised in Terah's house,
Driven by a more artful wit and the spirit of Jubal <note>In the Book of Genesis, Jubal is identified as the ancestor of all those who play the harp and flute; he is considered the patron of music.</note>,
Who knew how to join thin reeds together with wax,
Thus began his field-song, while everyone kept silent,
The animals listened, and the wind itself gained ears:

<margin>Shepherd’s song on the birth.</margin>
Rejoice, Heaven; earth, rejoice, and hear my field-song!
To the great Shepherd a young Son is born!
Rejoice, youths, maidens, rejoice, the fields, and the cattle,
The forests, hills, and valleys rejoice along;
For valleys, hills, and forests, cattle, and fields,
Yes, earth and heaven, now stand to herald Abraham’s blessing,
And full joy, saved for his old age,
Now that beautiful Sarah has borne him a Descendant!
The sun rises more cheerfully. The hilltop peaks blush.
The gentle breezes breathe a lovely scent of roses.
The meadows open up. The hawthorn blooms.
The olive tree drips with oil <note>original: "vet" (fat/richness). This refers to the abundance of the olive harvest, often used in the Bible as a symbol of divine favor.</note>, while the oak flows with honey.
The ear of corn gilds the field more than before;
Now that a Descendant is born to great Abraham.
How cheerfully the spring rustles! How the silver brook flows!
How unconcerned the flock grazes through the region,
Safe from cruel wolves and forest monsters!
Let no beast approach here: the place is holy!
For God has saved this blessed time for us,
Now that fertile Sarah has borne a young Son.

<summary>A lyrical passage where Elihu, a musically gifted youth, performs a pastoral song celebrating the birth of Isaac. The song portrays nature itself—the sun, plants, and animals—reacting with supernatural joy and abundance to the birth of Abraham's heir.</summary>

This is the blessing, which Heaven long predicted:
This is the promised Seed (as the man told us,
Who provides for the Lord’s house <note>Likely referring to Eliezer of Damascus, Abraham's chief steward, who managed the household's affairs.</note>) from whom one awaits <term>Goël</term> <note>original: "Goël". A Hebrew term for a "Kinsman-Redeemer." In Christian tradition, this was often interpreted as a prophecy of the Messiah.</note>,
The great Redeemer of the human race,
The Banisher of the Curse, the Reconciler of God’s wrath:
So great a Son is now born to Abraham.

This is the blessed Seed, from which a whole line
Of Princes shall sprout according to the ancient prophecy;
A people, that shall enjoy Canaan as their own,
From where the Euphrates comes shooting from the high mountains,
To where the Nile <note>original: "d'Egiptus". Here, the poet uses the name of the country to refer to its great river, the Nile, defining the traditional borders of the Promised Land.</note> carries its river-moisture downward:
So great a Descendant has Lady Sarah now brought forth.

Innumerable are the stars at night, in the halls of heaven,
The leaves of the trees, and the grass in these valleys:
Innumerable is the dust of the earth, and the sand of the sea;
But so innumerable may the lineage of my Lord be also:
Yes, such a blessing has the All-Blesser bestowed,
The long-promised Son, born to Lord Abraham.

Sooner shall the meadow miss the grass, the grass the morning dew;
The mountains the valleys, and the valleys their dark shadow:
Sooner shall the sun lack light, the moon her glow:
Sooner shall the mountain-stream return to the Lebanon <note>A poetic way of describing an impossibility—a river flowing backward up a mountain.</note>;
Sooner than the blessed lineage of my Lord shall die from the earth,
From this Descendant, whom Sarah has now brought forth.

Grow then, O young Boy, by Heaven’s mild blessing:
Just like the youthful grass in sunshine and rain:

<margin>Sarah’s rapture over the name Isaac.</margin>
Indeed, whoever from age to age shall believe this miracle,
Shall laugh with me, through a heavenly joy, poured from above
Into human hearts, like a laughter,
When all the kindred of the earth shall be blessed in him <note>A reference to the divine promise that through Abraham’s lineage—specifically Isaac—all nations would be blessed.</note>.
Who ever thought—if God’s words had not foretold it—
That anyone would still speak to my Abraham of Sarah:
"She suckled sons"; for I, though old and bent,
Have borne him a Son in his old age.
This has been brought to pass by God, and is a wonder in our eyes.

<margin>Hagar’s envy and Ishmael’s delusion.</margin>
But meanwhile, Lady Hagar finds herself deceived
In her hope, and lacks only joy in her heart.
Envy nourishes, at the very least, a secret pain,
And grows the more Sarah’s Son seems to grow:
She hides the flames that glow within her bosom.
But Ishmael, a lad over fifteen years old,
Of a wild nature, like a donkey in the forest <note>original: "ezel in het wout". This alludes to Genesis 16:12, where Ishmael is described as a "wild ass of a man."</note>,
Soon learns to read the passion from his mother’s eyes;
And notices the aversion and the disdain in Sarah’s being;
While his self-love imagines him as the eldest Son
Of Father Abraham, and he flatters himself with that privilege.
Presumptuous youth knows how to flatter itself with idle appearances.

Pressed by self-interest, breaks and bursts;
Long forced into the woman’s heart, it now discharges itself,
Like a storm breaking out, in the very presence of the guests:
